> Subject: Final Demand for Resolution - FCRA §605B

> Dear Mr. Cartwright,
> This is my final, good-faith demand for complete resolution and confidential settlement
> regarding TransUnion’s repeated, willful violations of the Fair Credit Reporting Act
> (FCRA), specifically §605B, §611(a)(6), §1681n, and §1681o, as well as violations of active
> FTC and CFPB Consent Orders (2023).
> Legal Standard (FCRA §605B and Related Provisions)
> Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) (FCRA §605B), CRAs must block fraudulent information within
> four (4) business days upon receiving:
> 12195064.1.3-10
> ● An FTC identity theft report
> ● Proof of identity
> ● Proof of address
> ● A signed statement identifying the fraudulent data
> Failure to act requires a written notice of denial with reasons under 15 U.S.C.
> §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii) (FCRA §611).
> Willful violations trigger statutory and punitive damages under §1681n. Negligent violations are
> actionable under §1681o.
> Timeline of §605B Submissions (Fully Documented)
> Since June 5, 2025, I submitted multiple complete §605B identity theft block requests, each
> via trackable certified mail with full documentation + signature of receipt, including:
> ● FTC Report
> ● Notarized Affidavit
> ● Government ID
> ● Proof of Address
> Submission Dates:
> ● June 9, 2025 (CFPB Complaint 250609-21400333)
> ● June 13, 2025
> ● June 16, 2025 (CFPB Complaint #250616-21593904)
> ● July 4th, 2025 (post CFPB Complaint #250704-22063859)
> Despite meeting every statutory requirement, TransUnion refused to block fraudulent data
> within 4 business days as required by §605B(a).
> Denial Reasons Provided by TransUnion
> (Calls have been recorded, documented, and transcribed via Minnesota 1 way consent laws.
> Although as a courtesy, all supervisors were informed beforehand of the recording &
> documentation of the calls)
> In calls and responses, TransUnion representatives provided unlawful reasons for refusing my
> §605B requests:
> 1. Creditor deletion letters required:
> 12195064.1.4-10
> ○ Repeatedly demanded letters from creditors as the “only” way, even though
> FCRA §605B has no such requirement.
> 2. “System will not allow” removals:
> ○ Special Handling Supervisor(s), alleged the system prevented deletion even with
> valid documentation.
> 3. Internal policy overrides law:
> ○ Employees cited “internal policy” to ignore §605B’s 4-day block requirement.
> 4. Rejecting valid FTC Reports and affidavits:
> ○ No deficiencies in my documentation were ever identified.
> ○ Rejecting Valid FTC Reports and Affidavits Without Lawful Justification
> Despite my submission of a valid FTC Identity Theft Report, sworn
> affidavit, and all required supporting documents under FCRA §605B,
> TransUnion has repeatedly rejected my request to block fraudulent
> inquiries without identifying any specific deficiency in the materials
> provided - either in writing or during recorded phone calls with supervisors
> from their Fraud Department.
> This violates multiple provisions of federal law:
> 1. FCRA §605B(b)(2):
> "An identity theft report may be a report to a law enforcement
> agency or the Federal Trade Commission."
> This statute explicitly confirms that a FTC Identity Theft Report
> (Affidavit) is legally valid for identity theft blocking requests. It does not
> require a police report, nor may the CRA reject an FTC report based
> solely on form preference.
> 2. FCRA §605B(c)(2):
> "The consumer reporting agency may not refuse to block the
> information on the grounds that the consumer has not provided any
> additional documentation or information, other than as described in
> subsection (b)."
> Once the FTC Report, affidavit, ID, and proof of address are submitted
> - which I provided in full - TransUnion is legally prohibited from requiring
> any additional documents or imposing further barriers.
> 12195064.1.5-10
> 3. FCRA §611(a)(6)(B)(iii): Requirement to Identify
> Basis for Denial
> If a CRA rejects or modifies a request, they are required to
> provide a specific explanation for the decision.
> "If the agency determines that the dispute is frivolous
> or irrelevant, it shall notify the consumer of the
> determination not later than 5 business days... and
> shall include the reasons for the determination, and
> identify any information required to investigate the
> disputed information."
> TransUnion has never identified a single specific reason for
> denying my requests. Instead, they issued vague blanket
> rejections and refused to cite any statutory basis, which
> violates both 605B and the notice requirements of 611(a).
> 4. Repeated Non-Disclosure and Obstruction
> (Documented Behavior):
> ● Phone Calls: On multiple calls (including with supervisors
> “Derek” and “Frank” in the Fraud and Special Handling
> departments), I explicitly asked:
> “What specific requirement is missing or incorrect in my
> FTC affidavit or documentation?”
> They refused to answer and declined to read or cite the
> specific reason, stating only:
> “It was rejected by another department.” or “We need
> creditor letters.”
> ● Written Notices: TransUnion’s written responses also
> failed to specify any deficiency, instead returning vague
> statements such as “the documentation is not acceptable” -
> without identifying what was missing, or how it deviated
> from 605B standards.
> This constitutes a willful evasion of legal duties, as federal law

> 12195064.1.6-10

> ○ My account was locked, obstructing my ability to dispute or manage freezes.

> This is a systemic pattern of disregard, not isolated mistakes:
> ● Employees use illegal pretexts (creditor letters, internal policies) to override §605B.
> ● Certified mail packets ignored, even after CFPB complaints.
> ● Online account blocked instead of complying.

> Damages and Exposure

> ○ 40–50 discrete willful violations documented.
> ○ Under FCRA §1681n, each violation is $1,000.

> Dear TransUnion Legal Department,
> This letter constitutes a good-faith, formal attempt to resolve repeated violations of my federal rights
> under the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA), specifically 15 U.S.C. § 1681c-2 (Section 605B) and §
> 1681i/§ 611, regarding TransUnion's refusal to block and delete unauthorized hard inquiries associated
> with a documented identity theft event.
> Summary of Violations (Documented & Recorded)
> Between AND BEFORE June 1–12, 2025, and on multiple prior occasions, I submitted over ten (10+)
> requests to your Fraud and Special Handling Team—including direct contact with Derek, Frank, Kayli,
> and more—using mail, fax, and recorded phone calls. Each request included:
> ● Valid FTC Identity Theft Report (§ 605B-compliant)
> ● Signed Written Statement of identity theft
> ● Government-issued ID + Proof of Address (utility bill)
> Despite submitting complete and legally sufficient documentation, your team:
> 1. Failed & REFUSED to block inquiries within 4 business days – violating § 605B(a)
> 2. Falsely claimed a police report was required, contrary to FTC guidance
> 3. Failed to provide reasons for denial – in violation of § 611(a)(6)(B)(iii)
> 4. Rejected packets on internal grounds (e.g., “arbitrary expiration dates for the FTC reports, where
> FTC states they do NOT expire; and 1 claim per FTC report at a time”) unsupported by law
> 5. Repeatedly refused corrective action despite recorded supervisory confirmation of receipt
> These actions indicate:
> ● Willful noncompliance (§ 1681n)
> ● Reckless disregard for FCRA obligations
> ● Negligent handling under § 1681o
> Systemic procedural abuse not based in statute or regulation
> 11830966.1.2-2
> Legal Exposure Summary
> Statute Violation Remedy
> § 1681n(a)(1)(A) Willful Noncompliance (10+ instances) $1,000 per violation = $10,000.00+
> § 1681n(a)(3) Reckless Disregard Punitive damages (TBD at trial)
> § 1681o Negligent Noncompliance Actual damages + legal fees
> Emotional Harm Denial of credit, stress, lost opportunities Estimated: $5,000–$15,000
> Estimated Exposure Range:
> Minimum: $15,000.00
> Maximum (with punitive): $50,000.00+
> Settlement Proposal – Without Prejudice
> To resolve this matter amicably and avoid formal legal action, I am willing to settle under the following
> terms:
> 1. Monetary Settlement: $17,500.00 if paid within 10 business days (by 06/22/2025)
> 2. 3. 4. Immediate Action: All unauthorized inquiries permanently blocked and deleted per § 605B
> within 4 business days
> Written Acknowledgment: Confirm that an FTC Identity Theft Report satisfies 605B
> requirements and police reports are not required
> Proper training and handling for Transunion reps and supervisors to comply with federal law and
> regulations when it comes to 605B claims (Special handling department & fraud department)
> Next Steps and Legal Counsel Consideration

> TransUnion’s response is factually incorrect, legally deficient, and directly
> contradicts the plain language of FCRA § 605B. The company is
> misrepresenting both my documentation and their statutory obligations,
> relying on non-statutory “internal policies” to override federal law. Their
> denial is a textbook case of willful noncompliance and reckless disregard
> under 15 U.S.C. §§ 1681n-o. Key Points: Misrepresentation of
> Documentation TransUnion falsely claims my FTC Identity Theft Report
> and notarized affidavit were “not acceptable.” All documents met FCRA §
> 605B(b)(1)-(3) requirements and were acknowledged as received and valid
> by multiple TransUnion representatives. Failure to Block Within 4 Business
> Days § 605B(a) mandates blocking within 4 business days of receiving
> required documentation-no exceptions for “policy” or “investigation.”
> Instead of blocking, TransUnion chose to “contact creditors,” which is not
> permitted as a substitute for blocking. Use of Non-Statutory Internal
> Policies TransUnion’s reliance on undisclosed “internal policies” is a
> deceptive practice under FTC Act § 5 (UDAP). Federal law supersedes
> internal company rules; citing such rules to deny a statutory right is
> unlawful. Pattern of Willful Noncompliance Four supervisors, across
> multiple dates (June 5–June 17), repeated the same unlawful refusal despite
> being informed of FCRA requirements. Each refusal constitutes a separate
> willful violation. Statutory Exposure 20-40+ discrete violations of § 605B ×
> $1,000 statutory damages under § 1681n = $20,000-$40,000+, plus punitive

> USPS CERTIFIED MAIL
> 9214 8901 4298 0426 8405 88
> Final Pre-Litigation Demand - Fair Credit
> Billing Act Violations
> Date: 09/11/2025
> To: Navy Federal Credit Union - Executive Resolutions & Legal Department
> CC: Compliance Department; CFPB Case #250825-23483188 & #250828-23580468; NCUA;
> Visa Compliance
> Subject: Final Pre-Litigation Demand - $20,600 Billing Error Dispute
> Dear Navy Federal,
> This letter serves as my final pre-litigation demand regarding Navy Federal’s mishandling of my
> $20,600 dispute with Credit Counsel Elite. If this matter is not resolved on the terms below, I will
> file suit in U.S. District Court on September 26, 2025 and pursue all remedies available under
> the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), the FTC Act,
> state UDAP statutes, and Visa Core Rules.
> Navy Federal has also failed to provide the required written explanation of its
> denial, itself a direct violation of FCBA §1666 and Reg Z §1026.13(f).
> Documented Violations by Navy Federal
> 1. Failure to Conduct Reasonable Investigation
> ○ Violation of FCBA §1666(a) and Reg Z §1026.13(c) by disregarding evidence:
> payment processed at 4:42 PM before contract disclosure at 4:46 PM, fraudulent
> $300K-$500K earnings inducements, and retaliatory revocation of access.
> ○ A form denial based solely on merchant policy is not a “reasonable investigation”
> under the statute.
> 2. Unlawful Collection Activity During Dispute
> 12428080.1.2-6
> ○ Violation of FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B)(ii) by re-posting the $20,600 charge, accruing
> interest, and reporting utilization while the billing error claim was unresolved.
> 3. Non-Delivery of Services
> ○ Violation of FCBA §1666(b)(3) where services were not delivered as agreed at
> the time of the transaction. Payment was taken prior to disclosure, course
> content instructed unlawful misrepresentation, and access was revoked in
> retaliation.
> 4. Failure to Apply Visa Core Rules (12.5.3.2)
> ○ Navy Federal failed to honor mandatory chargeback rights for
> misrepresentation/non-delivery, which apply regardless of merchant refund
> policies or “portal access.”
> 5. Improper Deference to Merchant Policy Despite Fraud
> ○ By deferring to Credit Counsel Elite’s “no-refund” terms, Navy Federal is
> effectively shielding a merchant engaged in fraudulent inducement and unlawful
> training.
> ○ FCBA §1666 requires independent investigation; Visa rules override merchant
> policies; and contracts formed under fraud and lack of mutual assent are void ab
> initio.
> 6. Failure to Provide Written Explanation of Denial
> ● FCBA §1666(a)(3)(B): Requires creditors who deny a billing error to provide a written
> explanation or clarification of reasons.
> ● Reg Z §1026.13(f): Requires issuers to mail/deliver that explanation and provide
> supporting documents upon request.
> ● Navy Federal’s Failure: Navy’s response consisted only of a conclusory “no billing error
> occurred,” with no reasoning and no engagement with the evidence provided.
> ● Conclusion: This omission is a direct statutory violation, further demonstrating willful
> noncompliance and bad faith handling of the dispute.
> Damages and Litigation Exposure
> If litigation is required, I will seek:
> ● Refund of $20,600 - immediate billing correction.
> ● Statutory Damages: up to $10,000 under FCBA/Reg Z.
> ● Actual Damages: unlawful interest, credit harm, and consequential costs
> ($2,000–$10,000+).
> ● Punitive Damages: for willful mishandling and reckless disregard ($10,000–$20,000+).
> ● Attorney’s Fees & Costs: fully recoverable under FCBA if counsel is engaged.
> Total Litigation Exposure: $67,000–$120,000+ in addition to the $20,600 refund, plus
> reputational risk, regulatory referral, and Visa compliance penalties.
> Settlement Proposal
> To resolve this matter without escalation, I will accept settlement under the following terms:
> 1. 2. 3. 4. 5. Immediate refund of $20,600 (or restoration of permanent credit).
> Waiver of all accrued interest and fees.
> Correction of any adverse credit reporting/utilization.
> Additional immediate $10,000 in settlement for statutory damages, punitive exposure,
> and the hardship, stress, and time expended due to Navy Federal’s mishandling.
> Written assurance of no retaliation – no closure, restriction, or adverse action against my
> Navy Federal accounts or membership for exercising my rights under FCBA and Reg Z.
> Consequences of Non-Settlement
> 12428080.1.3-6
> ● Acknowledgment Required: Please acknowledge receipt of this letter and provide the
> assigned Executive Resolutions case ID within 48 hours.
> ● Settlement Deadline: If Navy Federal fails to comply with the above terms by 5:00 PM
> CT on September 25, 2025 (10 business days), I will immediately file suit on September
> 26, 2025, and:
> ○ File supplemental complaints with CFPB, NCUA, FTC, State Attorney General,
> and request Visa Compliance enforcement;
> ○ Submit this letter to the court as evidence of willful noncompliance and bad faith.
> Litigation Hold / Preservation Notice
> This letter also serves as a litigation hold demand. Navy Federal must preserve all records
> related to this dispute, including internal case notes, call recordings, escalation routing,
> merchant communications, and Visa compliance determinations. Failure to preserve will be
> treated as spoliation and presented to the court.
> Conclusion
> ● See Attachment A - Navy Federal Rebuttal Matrix for a detailed breakdown
> of anticipated defenses and why they fail under FCBA, Reg Z, and Visa
> rules.
> I value my membership and would prefer cooperative resolution. However, given the scope of
> these violations and the regulatory obligations at stake, settlement on the above terms is the
> only acceptable resolution. Litigation will expose Navy Federal to damages several times
> greater than settlement and regulatory scrutiny that cannot be undone.
> Sincerely,
> Alan Nguyen
> 702 267 7179
> 11150 Rhode Island Ave S, Bloomington MN 55438
> 12428080.1.4-6
> Attachment A - Navy Federal Rebuttal
> Matrix
> Subject: Anticipated Defenses and Legal Responses
> Reference: $20,600 Billing Error Dispute – Credit Counsel Elite
> This matrix outlines likely defenses Navy Federal may raise and the corresponding multi-angle
> legal responses. Each defense is nullified under Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666),
> Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), the FTC Act (15 U.S.C. §45), state UDAP statutes, and Visa
> Core Rules (12.5.3.2).
> 1. “Portal access = delivery”
> Why It Fails:
> ● FCBA §1666(b)(3): Services not delivered as agreed at the time of the transaction =
> billing error.
> ● Visa Rule 12.5.3.2: Chargebacks required for misrepresentation or non-delivery
> regardless of portal access.
> ● FTC Act §45(a): Unlawful/deceptive services = non-delivery.
> ● Retaliation: Revoked access proves no bona fide delivery.
> Conclusion: Under FCBA §1666(b)(3) and Visa Rule 12.5.3.2, portal access—especially when
> later revoked or unlawful—cannot constitute delivery.
> 2. “You initialed the contract = consent”
> Why It Fails:
> ● Timeline Evidence: Payment 4:42 PM, terms disclosed 4:46 PM.
> ● Restatement (Second) Contracts §17 & UCC §2-204(1): Mutual assent must exist at
> formation.
> ● Fraudulent Inducement: Earnings misrepresentation voids contract.
> Conclusion: Because payment preceded disclosure, there was no contemporaneous assent.
> Under Restatement §17 and UCC §2-204(1), retroactive initials do not create assent. The
> contract is void for lack of mutual assent.
> 3. “No refund policy applies”
> Why It Fails:
> ● FCBA §1666(a): Issuer must investigate billing errors regardless of merchant policy.
> ● Visa Rule 12.5.3.2: Overrides refund/cancellation terms.
> ● FTC Act §45(a): Retaining funds obtained by deception is unlawful.
> ● Contract/Public Policy: Fraud vitiates refund restrictions.
> Conclusion: Merchant policies cannot override FCBA §1666 or Visa Rule 12.5.3.2. A no-refund
> clause tied to a fraudulent contract is unenforceable as against public policy.
> 4. “We relied on the merchant’s confirmation”
> Why It Fails:
> ● FCBA §1666(a): Requires issuer to perform a “reasonable investigation,” not adopt
> merchant statements.
> ● Reg Z §1026.13(f): Must provide a written explanation addressing the consumer’s
> evidence.
> ● CFPB/NCUA Guidance: Merchant reliance = noncompliance.
> 12428080.1.5-6
> Conclusion: Blind reliance violates FCBA §1666(a) and Reg Z §1026.13(f). Issuers must
> conduct an independent investigation; failure is a statutory violation.
> 5. “You completed the course content”
> Why It Fails:
> ● FTC Act §45(a): Unlawful training = non-delivery.
> ●

## SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES (IN SIMPLE TERMS)

### 2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under Federal Law (FCRA §605B)

When a consumer submits:
1. An **FTC Identity Theft Report** (or police report)
2. Proof of identity
3. Proof of address
4. A written statement identifying the fraudulent items

👉 **The credit bureau MUST block the fraudulent information within 4 business days.**

There is:
- ❌ No discretion  
- ❌ No investigation requirement  
- ❌ No creditor permission requirement  
- ❌ No police report requirement if an FTC report is provided  

This is not optional. It is mandatory federal law.

---

- ✅ Government-issued photo ID
- ✅ Social Security card (additional proof)
- ✅ Proof of address (utility bill)
- ✅ Written statement identifying fraudulent inquiries
- ✅ Certified mail with signature confirmation

No submission was missing anything.

# SECTION X — TRAN SUNION’S “COMPANY POLICY” DEFENSE  
## A TEXTBOOK CASE OF WILLFUL FCRA VIOLATION

---

## X.1 The Core Issue (Plain English)

TransUnion did **not** misunderstand the law.  
They **knowingly chose** to follow internal company policy **instead of federal statute**.

This is not negligence.  
This is **intentional lawbreaking**.

At multiple points, TransUnion representatives explicitly stated that:

- Their **internal system**
- Their **internal policy**
- Their **company procedures**

**prevented them from complying with FCRA §605B**, even after being informed—verbally and in writing—of the law’s requirements.

Federal law does not permit this.

---

## X.2 What the Law Says About “Company Policy”

### Federal Law Is Supreme

Under long-standing constitutional and statutory principles:

- **Federal statutes override internal corporate policy**
- **Internal procedures cannot narrow statutory rights**
- **Operational inconvenience is not a defense**

The FCRA contains **no carve-out** for:
- System limitations  
- Internal workflows  
- Fraud-department policies  
- “That’s how our system works”  

If a company’s system cannot comply with the law, **the system is unlawful**—not the consumer.

---

## X.3 How TransUnion Used “Policy” to Violate the Law

### Repeated Statements by TransUnion (Documented & Recorded)

TransUnion supervisors and representatives repeatedly stated variations of:

- “That’s not how our system works.”
- “Our system won’t allow us to remove inquiries this way.”
- “That’s not our policy.”
- “We need a creditor letter.”
- “You have to contact the creditor directly.”
- “This has to go through a different department.”
- “We can’t do this because your account is locked.”

Each of these statements is **legally irrelevant** under FCRA §605B.

Each one constitutes an **independent violation**.

---

## X.4 Why This Automatically Establishes WILLFUL Noncompliance

### Willfulness Standard (Safeco v. Burr)

A violation is “willful” if the defendant:
- Knows what the law requires **or**
- Acts in **reckless disregard** of statutory duties

TransUnion’s conduct satisfies **both**.

They were:
- Repeatedly informed of §605B
- Provided the statute verbatim
- Given CFPB complaints citing the law
- Given certified letters explaining the law
- Recorded acknowledging the request type

Yet they **continued to cite policy** as a reason to refuse compliance.

That is **reckless disregard by definition**.

---

## X.5 Internal Policy as Evidence of SYSTEMIC Misconduct

This was not a one-off error by a frontline agent.

Evidence shows:
- Multiple supervisors gave the same illegal explanation
- The same false requirements were repeated across weeks
- CFPB responses echoed the same policy-based excuses
- No one corrected the misstatement internally
- No compliance override occurred

This demonstrates:
- A **company-wide practice**
- A **standard operating procedure**
- A **systemic FCRA violation**

Courts treat this as **aggravating conduct**.

---

## X.6 Why “Company Policy” INCREASES Damages (Not Reduces Them)

### Courts View Policy-Over-Law As Worse Than Error

Mistake = possible negligence  
Policy = **intentional design choice**

When a company says:
> “We can’t comply because of policy”

They are effectively saying:
> “We have chosen not to comply with federal law.”

This supports:
- Punitive damages
- Injunctive relief
- Regulatory escalation
- Findings of reckless disregard

---

## X.7 Comparison to Prior TransUnion Cases

Courts have already ruled against TransUnion where:

- Internal processes overrode statutory duties
- Automated systems caused continued reporting
- Employees relied on “how the system works”

These cases resulted in:
- Large statutory awards
- Punitive damages
- Court-ordered compliance changes

This case fits squarely within that pattern.

---

## X.8 Why This Excuse Will Fail Instantly in Court

A judge will ask one question:

> “Where in the statute does it say your policy controls?”

There is no answer.

Once TransUnion admits:
- “This is our policy”
- “The system won’t allow it”

The legal analysis is complete.

Liability attaches.

This case is not about:

That is the definition of **willful noncompliance** under the FCRA.

---

## 5.2 What Federal Law Says About “Company Policy”

Federal law does **not** permit a consumer reporting agency to:
- Narrow statutory rights
- Delay mandatory action
- Add requirements
- Refuse compliance

There is **no exception** in the FCRA for:
- Internal systems  
- Fraud department workflows  
- “That’s how our policy works”  
- “The system won’t allow it”  

If a company’s system cannot comply with federal law, **the system is unlawful** — not the consumer’s request.

---

## 5.3 Documented “Policy Over Law” Statements (Repeated)

TransUnion supervisors and representatives repeatedly stated:

- “That’s not how our system works.”  
- “Our system won’t allow us to remove inquiries this way.”  
- “That’s not our policy.”  
- “We need a creditor deletion letter.”  
- “You must contact the creditor.”  
- “This has to go through another department.”  
- “Your account is locked, so we can’t do this.”

Each of these statements is **legally irrelevant** under FCRA §605B.

Each statement represents a **knowing refusal to comply**.

---

## 5.4 Why This Automatically Proves WILLFUL NONCOMPLIANCE

### Governing Standard: *Safeco v. Burr*

A violation is willful if the defendant:
- Knows what the law requires **or**
- Acts in reckless disregard of the statute

Here:
- TransUnion was informed of §605B repeatedly
- The statute was cited verbatim
- CFPB complaints referenced the law
- Certified letters explained the law

Despite this, TransUnion **continued to apply internal policy instead of federal law**.

That is **reckless disregard as a matter of law**.

- Multiple supervisors gave the same unlawful explanation

- A **company-wide practice**
- A **standard operating procedure**
- A **systemic violation of the FCRA**

Mistakes may support negligence.

# GRAND MASTER CASE DOCUMENT  
## Alan Nguyen v. TransUnion LLC  
### Willful, Reckless, and Negligent Violations of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA)

---

## SECTION 1 — EXECUTIVE OVERVIEW (PLAIN ENGLISH)

### 1.1 What Happened

I was the victim of identity theft.  
Federal law gives identity theft victims **mandatory protections**.

I complied with the law **fully, repeatedly, and precisely**.  
TransUnion did not.

Despite receiving **complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity-theft documentation multiple times**, TransUnion refused to remove fraudulent inquiries, invented requirements that do not exist in law, misrepresented federal statutes, admitted they did not investigate, blocked access to my credit file, and continued reporting known-fraudulent information for months.

This conduct violates the Fair Credit Reporting Act repeatedly and willfully.

---

### 1.2 What This Case Is *Not* About

This case is **not** about:
- Missing documents
- Confusion
- Delay
- Discretion
- Clerical error

This case **is** about:
- A national credit bureau
- Knowing what federal law requires
- Choosing internal company policy instead

---

## SECTION 2 — GOVERNING LAW (FOUNDATION)

### 2.1 Identity Theft Blocking — FCRA §605B  
**15 U.S.C. §1681c-2**

When a consumer submits:
1. An FTC Identity Theft Report **or** police report  
2. Proof of identity  
3. Proof of address  
4. A written statement identifying the fraudulent items  

The consumer reporting agency **MUST**:

- Block the fraudulent information
- Within **4 business days**
- Without investigation
- Without creditor permission
- Without additional documentation

There are **no exceptions**.

---

### 2.2 Enforcement & Liability

- **§1681n** — Willful or reckless noncompliance  
  - Statutory damages
  - Punitive damages
  - Fees and costs  

- **§1681o** — Negligent noncompliance  
  - Actual damages  

Federal law **preempts internal corporate policy**.

---

## SECTION 3 — PLAINTIFF’S FULL COMPLIANCE (STEP BY STEP)

### 3.1 Documentation Submitted (EVERY TIME)

Each submission contained **all legally required materials**:

- FTC Identity Theft Report (federal form)
- Notarized identity theft affidavit
- Government-issued photo ID
- Social Security card (additional proof)
- Proof of address (utility bill)
- Written statement identifying fraudulent inquiries
- Certified mail with signature confirmation

No submission was deficient.

- No blocking within 4 business days
- No blocking for months
- Continued reporting after notice

### 4.2 Illegal Documentation Demands
TransUnion demanded:
- Creditor deletion letters
- Police reports instead of FTC reports
- One packet per inquiry
- Internal “system” requirements

These demands are **explicitly forbidden by §605B**.

---

### 4.3 Misrepresentation of Law (Recorded)
Supervisors stated:
- “That’s not our policy”
- “Our system won’t allow it”
- “You must contact the creditor”
- “We have not investigated”

These are **knowing misstatements**.

GRAND MASTER CASE DOCUMENT
Alan Nguyen v. TransUnion LLC
Willful, Reckless, and Negligent Violations of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA)

SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)
1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)
I was the victim of identity theft.
Federal law gives identity theft victims clear, mandatory protections.
I followed the law exactly.
TransUnion did not.
Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:
Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries
Invented documentation requirements that do not exist
Misrepresented federal law
Admitted they did not investigate
Locked me out of my own credit file
Continued reporting known-fraudulent information for months
This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)
I respectfully request:
Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)
Restoration of full TransUnion account access under 15 U.S.C. §1681g
Written confirmation of:
Inquiry deletions
Account restoration
Staff retraining on FCRA §605B
Elimination of any internal policies or systems that override federal law or delay the mandatory four-business-day blocking requirement
Confidential financial settlement of $75,000–$100,000, reflecting the full scope of documented violations and damages
This proposal is made without prejudice and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.
Proposed Acceptance Language
“TransUnion acknowledges receipt of this demand. As resolution, we have removed the disputed inquiries, restored account access, retrained applicable staff, and offer a confidential financial settlement of $____ in full satisfaction of all claims.”

SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES (IN SIMPLE TERMS)
2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under Federal Law (FCRA §605B)
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, when a consumer submits:
An FTC Identity Theft Report (or police report)
Proof of identity
Proof of address
A written statement identifying the fraudulent items
The consumer reporting agency MUST block the fraudulent information within four (4) business days.
There is:
No discretion
No investigation requirement
No creditor permission requirement
No police report requirement when an FTC report is provided
This duty is mandatory, not optional.

SECTION 3 — WHAT I DID (FULL COMPLIANCE, STEP BY STEP)
3.1 Documentation Submitted (Every Time)
Each submission contained all legally required materials:
FTC Identity Theft Report (official federal form)
Notarized identity theft affidavit
Government-issued photo ID
Social Security card (additional verification)
Proof of address (bank statement / utility bill)
Written statement identifying fraudulent inquiries
Certified mail with signature confirmation
No submission was incomplete or deficient.

SECTION 4 — WHAT TRANSUNION DID WRONG (FACTS ONLY)
4.1 Refused to Block Fraudulent Inquiries
No blocking within four days
No blocking for months
Continued reporting after notice
Violation of 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)

4.2 Demanded Documents the Law Does Not Allow
TransUnion repeatedly demanded:
Creditor deletion letters
Police reports
One packet per inquiry
Internal “system limitations”
Violation of 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2)

4.3 Misrepresented the Law (Recorded)
Supervisors stated:
“We need a creditor letter”
“The system won’t allow it”
“You must contact the creditor”
“We have not investigated”
These statements demonstrate knowing or reckless misconduct.

4.4 Failed to Explain Denials
Written responses stated only:
“Documents not acceptable”
TransUnion:
Did not identify deficiencies
Explicitly refused to explain how to cure
Did not cite any statute
Violation of 15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)

4.5 Locked Me Out of My Credit File
Online account permanently locked
Lock used to deny §605B rights
Refused to restore access despite verification
Violation of 15 U.S.C. §1681g

## SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)

### 1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)

I was the victim of identity theft.  
Federal law provides clear, mandatory protections to identity theft victims.

I followed the law exactly.  
TransUnion did not.

Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:

- Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries  
- Invented documentation requirements that do not exist in law  
- Misrepresented federal law to me  
- Admitted on recorded calls that they did not investigate  
- Locked me out of my own credit file  
- Continued reporting information they knew was fraudulent for months  

This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

---

### 1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)

I respectfully request:

1. Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)**  
2. Restoration of full TransUnion account access under **15 U.S.C. §1681g**  
3. Written confirmation of:
   - Inquiry deletions  
   - Account restoration  
   - Staff retraining on FCRA §605B  
   - Elimination of internal policies or system constraints that override federal law or delay the mandatory four-business-day blocking requirement  
4. A confidential financial settlement of **$75,000–$100,000**, reflecting the documented violations and damages  

This proposal is made **without prejudice** and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.

**Proposed Acceptance Language**  
“TransUnion acknowledges receipt of this demand. As resolution, we have removed the disputed inquiries, restored account access, retrained applicable staff, and offer a confidential financial settlement of $____ in full satisfaction of all claims.”

---

## SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES (IN SIMPLE TERMS)

### 2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under Federal Law (FCRA §605B)

Under **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2**, when a consumer submits:

- An FTC Identity Theft Report (or police report)  
- Proof of identity  
- Proof of address  
- A written statement identifying the fraudulent items  

A consumer reporting agency **must block** the fraudulent information **within four (4) business days**.

There is:
- No discretion  
- No investigation requirement  
- No creditor permission requirement  
- No police report requirement when an FTC report is provided  

This duty is mandatory and absolute.

---

## SECTION 3 — PLAINTIFF’S FULL COMPLIANCE

### 3.1 Documentation Submitted (Every Time)

Each submission contained all legally required materials:

- FTC Identity Theft Report (official federal form)  
- Notarized identity theft affidavit  
- Government-issued photo ID  
- Social Security card (additional verification)  
- Proof of address  
- Written statement identifying fraudulent inquiries  
- Certified mail with signature confirmation  

No submission was incomplete or deficient.

---

### 3.2 Timeline of Submissions

June 9, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion — CFPB ID + receipt  
June 13, 2025 — Certified Mail — TransUnion Legal Department — Signature proof  
June 16, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion Fraud Department — Signature proof  
July 4, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion — Signature proof  
August 7, 2025 — Certified Mail — TransUnion President — Signature proof  

Each submission restarted the four-business-day statutory clock.

---

## SECTION 4 — TRANSUNION’S MISCONDUCT (FACTS ONLY)

### 4.1 Failure to Block Fraudulent Inquiries  
Violation of **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)**

- No blocking within four business days  
- No blocking for months  
- Continued reporting after notice  

---

### 4.2 Unlawful Documentation Demands  
Violation of **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2)**

TransUnion repeatedly demanded:
- Creditor deletion letters  
- Police reports  
- One packet per inquiry  
- Compliance with internal system limitations  

Federal law explicitly forbids these demands.

---

### 4.3 Misrepresentation of Federal Law (Recorded)

Supervisors stated:
- “We need a creditor letter”  
- “The system won’t allow it”  
- “You must contact the creditor”  
- “We have not investigated”  

These statements constitute knowing or reckless misconduct.

---

### 4.4 Failure to Provide Lawful Denial Explanations  
Violation of **15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)**

TransUnion:
- Responded only “documents not acceptable”  
- Refused to identify deficiencies  
- Refused to explain how to cure  
- Cited no statute  

---

### 4.5 Denial of Access to Consumer File  
Violation of **15 U.S.C. §1681g**

- Account permanently locked  
- Lock used as justification to deny §605B rights  
- Access not restored despite identity verification  

---

### 4.6 Reliance on Internal Policy and System Limitations

TransUnion repeatedly relied on internal policies and technical constraints to override mandatory federal statutory duties.

Reliance on company policy or system limitations to defeat FCRA obligations constitutes **willful noncompliance as a matter of law**.

---

### 4.7 Context of TransUnion’s Data Breach History

TransUnion has suffered multiple widely reported data breaches and cybersecurity incidents involving unauthorized access to consumer data.

This history is relevant because:
- It demonstrates longstanding weaknesses in data integrity and safeguards  
- It heightens TransUnion’s duty to ensure accuracy and prompt correction  
- Continued reporting of known fraudulent data after notice reflects reckless disregard for consumer rights under **15 U.S.C. §1681e**  

---

## SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (SUMMARY)

Counts 1–25 — Failure to block after valid §605B submissions  
Counts 26–37 — Illegal documentation demands  
Counts 38–47 — Failure to provide lawful denial reasons  
Counts 48–52 — Denial of file access  
Counts 53–57 — Failure to maintain reasonable accuracy  
Counts 58–62 — Failure to investigate  

**Total Counts: 62**

Each count represents a separate statutory violation.

---

All counts arise under **15 U.S.C. §§1681c-2, 1681e, 1681g, and 1681i**.

SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)
1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)
I was the victim of identity theft.
Federal law provides clear, mandatory protections to identity theft victims.
I followed the law exactly.
TransUnion did not.
Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:
Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries
Invented documentation requirements that do not exist in law
Misrepresented federal law to me
Admitted on recorded calls that they did not investigate
Locked me out of my own credit file
Continued reporting information they knew was fraudulent for months
This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)

Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)
Restoration of full TransUnion account access under 15 U.S.C. §1681g

Inquiry deletions
Account restoration
Staff retraining on FCRA §605B
Elimination of internal policies or system constraints that override federal law or delay the mandatory four-business-day blocking requirement
A confidential financial settlement of $75,000–$100,000, reflecting the documented violations and damages
This proposal is made without prejudice and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.
Proposed Acceptance Language

SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES (IN SIMPLE TERMS)
2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under Federal Law (FCRA §605B)
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, when a consumer submits:
An FTC Identity Theft Report (or police report)
Proof of identity
Proof of address
A written statement identifying the fraudulent items
A consumer reporting agency must block the fraudulent information within four (4) business days.
There is:
No discretion
No investigation requirement
No creditor permission requirement
No police report requirement when an FTC report is provided
This duty is mandatory and absolute.

Certified mail with signature confirmation
No submission was incomplete or deficient.

June 16, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion Fraud Department — Signature proof

Failure to exercise reasonable care, even without intent.

## **SECTION 6 — LIABILITY STANDARD AND MENTAL STATE (WILLFUL, RECKLESS, AND NEGLIGENT NONCOMPLIANCE)**

The violations described herein constitute **willful noncompliance, reckless disregard, and, at minimum, negligent noncompliance** with the Fair Credit Reporting Act.

This conclusion is not based on inference or hindsight. It is compelled by the undisputed sequence of events, TransUnion’s own admissions, and the unambiguous statutory commands of the FCRA.

---

### **6.0.1 Willful Noncompliance (15 U.S.C. §1681n)**

TransUnion’s conduct satisfies the standard for **willful noncompliance** under **15 U.S.C. §1681n**, as interpreted by the United States Supreme Court in *Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr*.

Willfulness under the FCRA includes not only knowing violations, but also **reckless disregard of statutory duties**. A consumer reporting agency acts willfully when it is aware of its legal obligations—or of a substantial risk that its conduct violates the law—and proceeds anyway.

Here, TransUnion:

- Received **complete, legally sufficient identity theft documentation** on multiple occasions  
- Was placed on **repeated notice** of its mandatory duty under **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2**  
- Failed to block the fraudulent information within the four-business-day statutory deadline  
- Continued reporting information it knew, or was required by law to treat as, fraudulent  
- Repeated these violations **after notice**, **after CFPB complaints**, and **after certified mail to its legal department and executive leadership**

These are not isolated mistakes. They are **repeated, post-notice statutory violations**, which courts routinely recognize as willful noncompliance.

---

### **6.0.2 Reckless Disregard of Federal Law**

Even if TransUnion were to deny actual knowledge, the record independently establishes **reckless disregard**, which alone is sufficient to trigger willful liability under the FCRA.

TransUnion repeatedly justified its refusal to comply with §605B by citing:

- “Company policy”  
- “System limitations”  
- Internal operational constraints  
- The need for creditor letters or police reports not required by statute  

Reliance on internal policies or technical limitations to override **clear federal statutory mandates** constitutes reckless disregard as a matter of law.

The FCRA does not permit consumer reporting agencies to subordinate statutory duties to internal systems, policies, or convenience. When a regulated entity knowingly chooses internal process over federal law, it acts in reckless disregard of that law.

This conclusion is further reinforced by **recorded admissions** that:
- No investigation occurred, and  
- Compliance was refused due to internal limitations rather than legal authority.

---

### **6.0.3 Willful Misconduct (Institutional and Policy-Driven)**

While “willful misconduct” is not a separate statutory cause of action under the FCRA, it is a **critical factual characterization** that directly supports punitive damages and defeats any claim of good-faith error.

The record demonstrates institutional misconduct, including:

- Systematic refusal to comply with §605B despite repeated notice  
- Misrepresentation of federal law to the consumer  
- Policy-level decisions to deny relief not based on statutory criteria  
- Continued enforcement of unlawful requirements across multiple submissions  

This is not clerical error. It is **policy-driven noncompliance**.

Courts consistently recognize that when violations flow from internal policy rather than accident, punitive damages are appropriate.

---

### **6.0.4 Negligent Noncompliance (15 U.S.C. §1681o) — At Minimum**

Even if TransUnion were able to negate willfulness or recklessness—which the record does not support—its conduct **indisputably satisfies the standard for negligent noncompliance** under **15 U.S.C. §1681o**.

TransUnion failed to exercise reasonable care by:

- Continuing to report information after verified identity theft notice  
- Failing to maintain reasonable procedures to ensure accuracy  
- Failing to conduct any meaningful investigation after dispute and notice  

Negligence is therefore established as a minimum baseline. The evidence, however, far exceeds that threshold.

---

### **6.0.5 Summary of Liability Standard**

Based on the undisputed facts and statutory framework:

- **Negligence is unavoidable**
- **Reckless disregard is independently established**
- **Willful noncompliance is affirmatively demonstrated**

Accordingly, TransUnion is subject to liability under **15 U.S.C. §1681n**, including statutory damages, punitive damages, and attorneys’ fees.

The detailed counts set forth below should be evaluated under this liability framework.

# PART II — FEDERAL COMPLAINT COUNTS (PLEADING-READY)

You can paste the following directly into a complaint under “Causes of Action.”

---

## COUNT I  
### WILLFUL VIOLATION OF FCRA §605B  
**(15 U.S.C. §§1681c-2, 1681n)**

TransUnion received complete and legally sufficient identity theft documentation from Plaintiff on multiple occasions. Despite this notice, TransUnion failed to block the fraudulent inquiries within four business days as required by 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a).

TransUnion’s failures were repeated, post-notice, and continued for months. The statutory duty was clear and mandatory. TransUnion’s refusal to comply constitutes willful noncompliance under 15 U.S.C. §1681n.

Plaintiff seeks statutory damages, punitive damages, attorneys’ fees, and injunctive relief.

---

## COUNT II  
### WILLFUL VIOLATION — UNLAWFUL DOCUMENTATION DEMANDS  
**(15 U.S.C. §§1681c-2(c)(2), 1681n)**

TransUnion unlawfully conditioned identity theft blocking on documentation not permitted by statute, including creditor letters, police reports, and internal system requirements.

Federal law expressly prohibits such demands. TransUnion’s conduct was knowing, policy-driven, and willful.

---

## COUNT III  
### WILLFUL VIOLATION — FAILURE TO PROVIDE LAWFUL DENIAL EXPLANATIONS  
**(15 U.S.C. §§1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii), 1681n)**

TransUnion issued vague denials stating only “documents not acceptable,” while refusing to identify deficiencies, explain how to cure, or cite statutory authority.

This constitutes a willful violation of §611.

---

## COUNT IV  
### WILLFUL VIOLATION — DENIAL OF ACCESS TO CONSUMER FILE  
**(15 U.S.C. §§1681g, 1681n)**

TransUnion locked Plaintiff out of his consumer file and used that lockout to deny §605B relief, despite verified identity.

This constitutes a willful denial of access under §609.

---

## COUNT V  
### FAILURE TO MAINTAIN REASONABLE ACCURACY  
**(15 U.S.C. §§1681e, 1681n and/or 1681o)**

After receiving verified notice of identity theft, TransUnion continued reporting fraudulent information and failed to maintain reasonable procedures to ensure accuracy.

At minimum, this conduct was negligent. The record supports willfulness.

---

## COUNT VI  
### FAILURE TO INVESTIGATE  
**(15 U.S.C. §§1681i, 1681n and/or 1681o)**

TransUnion admitted on recorded calls that no investigation occurred. This constitutes a violation of §611 and supports liability under both negligent and willful standards.

---

# PART III — PUNITIVE DAMAGES ARGUMENT (STANDALONE, STRONG)

## PUNITIVE DAMAGES ARE WARRANTED

Punitive damages are appropriate where a defendant’s conduct reflects reckless or callous disregard for federally protected rights, or where violations are willful and policy-driven.

Here, punitive damages are warranted because:

1. **The statutory duty was unambiguous**  
   Section 605B mandates blocking within four business days. No discretion exists.

2. **Violations were repeated after notice**  
   TransUnion continued noncompliance after CFPB complaints, certified legal notice, and executive-level notice.

3. **Violations were justified by internal policy**  
   TransUnion repeatedly cited “system limitations” and “company policy” rather than law.

4. **Misrepresentations of law were made to the consumer**  
   Recorded calls show false statements regarding creditor letters, investigations, and statutory requirements.

5. **The conduct was institutional, not accidental**  
   The pattern reflects operational decision-making, not clerical error.

Courts routinely award punitive damages where a consumer reporting agency substitutes internal policy for federal law. A conservative punitive multiplier is therefore appropriate.

---

## CONCLUSION ON DAMAGES

TransUnion’s conduct justifies:
- Statutory damages under §1681n  
- Actual damages  
- Punitive damages  
- Attorneys’ fees and costs

## SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)

### 1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)

I was the victim of identity theft.  
Federal law provides clear, mandatory protections to identity theft victims.

I followed the law exactly.  
TransUnion did not.

Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:

- Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries  
- Invented documentation requirements that do not exist in law  
- Misrepresented federal law  
- Admitted on recorded calls that no investigation occurred  
- Locked me out of my own credit file  
- Continued reporting information it knew was fraudulent for months  

This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

---

### 1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)

I respectfully request:

1. Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)**  
2. Restoration of full TransUnion account access under **15 U.S.C. §1681g**  
3. Written confirmation of:
   - Inquiry deletions  
   - Account restoration  
   - Staff retraining on FCRA §605B  
   - Elimination of internal policies or system constraints overriding federal law  
4. Confidential settlement of **$75,000–$100,000**

This proposal is made **without prejudice** and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.

---

## SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES

### 2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under FCRA §605B

Under **15 U.S.C. §1681c-2**, upon receipt of:

- An FTC Identity Theft Report  
- Proof of identity  
- Proof of address  
- A written statement identifying fraudulent items  

A consumer reporting agency **must block** the fraudulent information **within four (4) business days**.

There is:
- No discretion  
- No investigation prerequisite  
- No creditor permission requirement  

This duty is mandatory and absolute.

---

## SECTION 3 — PLAINTIFF’S FULL COMPLIANCE

### 3.1 Documentation Submitted

Each submission included:
- FTC Identity Theft Report  
- Notarized affidavit  
- Government photo ID  
- Social Security card  
- Proof of address  
- Written identification of fraudulent inquiries  
- Certified mail with signature confirmation  

No submission was deficient.

### 3.2 Timeline (Abbreviated)

June 9, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion  
June 13, 2025 — Certified Mail — TU Legal  
June 16, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TU Fraud  
July 4, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion  
August 7, 2025 — Certified Mail — TU President  

Each restarted the four-business-day statutory clock.

---

## SECTION 4 — TRANSUNION’S MISCONDUCT

### 4.1 Failure to Block (§1681c-2)

- No blocking within four days  
- No blocking for months  
- Continued reporting after notice  

### 4.2 Unlawful Documentation Demands (§1681c-2(c)(2))

- Creditor letters  
- Police reports  
- One-packet-per-inquiry rules  
- System limitations  

All forbidden by statute.

### 4.3 Misrepresentation of Law (Recorded)

Statements included:
- “We need a creditor letter”
- “The system won’t allow it”
- “We did not investigate”

### 4.4 Failure to Provide Denial Explanations (§1681i)

- “Documents not acceptable”
- No deficiencies identified
- No cure explained
- No statute cited

### 4.5 Denial of File Access (§1681g)

- Account locked
- Lock used to deny §605B rights
- Access not restored despite verification

### 4.6 Policy & System Overrides

TransUnion substituted internal policy and system limitations for federal law.  
This constitutes willful noncompliance.

### 4.7 Data Breach Context

TransUnion’s publicly reported data breaches establish heightened foreseeability, duty of care, and the importance of accurate, prompt correction after identity theft notice. Continued reporting after notice reflects reckless disregard under **§1681e**.

---

## SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (SUMMARY)

- Counts 1–25 — Failure to block (§605B)  
- Counts 26–37 — Illegal documentation demands  
- Counts 38–47 — Denial notice failures (§611)  
- Counts 48–52 — File access denial (§609)  
- Counts 53–57 — Accuracy failures (§1681e)  
- Counts 58–62 — Failure to investigate (§1681i)  

**Total Counts: 62**

---

## SECTION 6 — LIABILITY STANDARD & DETAILED COUNT BREAKDOWN

### 6.0 Liability Standard (Willful, Reckless, Negligent)

TransUnion’s conduct constitutes **willful noncompliance, reckless disregard, and at minimum negligent noncompliance** under the FCRA.

Under *Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr*, willfulness includes reckless disregard of statutory duties. TransUnion received repeated notice of clear statutory obligations and chose internal policy over federal law.

Negligence is the minimum baseline; the evidence far exceeds it.

## SECTION 6 — LIABILITY STANDARD & DETAILED COUNT BREAKDOWN

### 6.0 Liability Standard (Willful, Reckless, and Negligent Noncompliance)

TransUnion’s conduct constitutes **willful noncompliance**, **reckless disregard**, and, at a minimum, **negligent noncompliance** under the Fair Credit Reporting Act.

Under **Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr**, willfulness includes conduct taken in **reckless disregard** of statutory duties. A company acts recklessly when it is aware of a substantial risk that its conduct violates federal law and proceeds anyway.

Here, TransUnion received **repeated notice** of:
- Plaintiff’s identity theft
- Complete and legally sufficient §605B documentation
- The mandatory four-business-day blocking requirement

Despite this notice, TransUnion **chose internal policy and system limitations over federal law**, continued reporting known fraudulent information, and admitted on recorded calls that no investigation occurred.

Negligence is the **minimum baseline**. The evidentiary record supports **willfulness and recklessness well beyond negligence**.

---

### 6.1 Counts 1–25 — Failure to Block Fraudulent Information  
**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) (FCRA §605B)

**Trigger:**  
Each failure to block fraudulent inquiries within four (4) business days following receipt of a complete identity theft submission.

**Relevant Submissions (Non-Exhaustive):**
- June 9, 2025
- June 13, 2025
- June 16, 2025
- July 4, 2025
- August 7, 2025

Each submission independently satisfied all statutory requirements and **restarted the four-business-day clock**.

**Conduct:**  
TransUnion:
- Failed to block fraudulent inquiries within four business days
- Failed to block them for **months**
- Continued reporting inquiries it knew were fraudulent

**Notice:**  
- CFPB complaints
- Certified mail with signature confirmation
- Direct submissions to Legal, Fraud, and Executive offices

**Mental State:**  
Willful and reckless.  
The failures occurred **after notice**, were **repeated**, and were **policy-driven**, not accidental.

Each missed statutory deadline constitutes a **separate violation**, resulting in **25 independent counts**.

---

### 6.2 Counts 26–37 — Unlawful Documentation Demands  
**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2)

**Trigger:**  
Each instance in which TransUnion conditioned §605B relief on documentation **not authorized by statute**.

**Conduct:**  
TransUnion demanded:
- Creditor deletion letters
- Police reports where an FTC report was provided
- One-packet-per-inquiry submissions
- Compliance with internal system limitations

**Legal Standard:**  
Federal law **expressly prohibits** consumer reporting agencies from imposing additional documentation requirements beyond those enumerated in §605B.

**Notice:**  
Plaintiff repeatedly advised TransUnion that the required documentation had already been submitted and was legally sufficient.

**Mental State:**  
Willful.  
These demands were not errors; they were **affirmative policy choices** in direct contradiction of the statute.

Each unlawful demand constitutes a **separate statutory violation**, resulting in **12 independent counts**.

---

### 6.3 Counts 38–47 — Failure to Provide Lawful Denial Explanations  
**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)

**Trigger:**  
Each denial or rejection of Plaintiff’s submissions without a legally compliant explanation.

**Conduct:**  
TransUnion responded with:
- “Documents not acceptable”
while:
- Failing to identify any deficiency
- Refusing to explain how to cure
- Citing no statutory authority

**Legal Requirement:**  
When denying a dispute or identity theft submission, TransUnion was required to:
- State the specific reason for denial
- Identify what information was missing (if any)
- Provide instructions to cure

**Notice:**  
Plaintiff repeatedly requested clarification and was explicitly refused.

**Mental State:**  
Willful.  
The refusal to explain denials was deliberate and systematic.

Each deficient denial constitutes a **separate violation**, resulting in **10 independent counts**.

---

### 6.4 Counts 48–52 — Denial of Access to Consumer File  
**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681g (FCRA §609)

**Trigger:**  
Each instance in which TransUnion denied Plaintiff access to his consumer file after identity verification.

**Conduct:**  
TransUnion:
- Permanently locked Plaintiff’s account
- Used the lock as justification to deny §605B rights
- Refused to restore access despite verified identity

**Legal Requirement:**  
Consumers have an **absolute right** to access their file upon proper identification.

**Notice:**

### Plain-Language Explanation of How the Violation Counts Are Calculated

This case does not rely on assumptions, estimates, or inflated numbers.  
Each violation counted in this document is tied to a **specific problem**, a **specific submission**, and a **specific legal deadline that TransUnion missed**.

Here is how the counting works in simple terms.

#### Step 1: How Many Fraud Problems Existed

Plaintiff identified **four specific fraudulent credit inquiries** on his TransUnion credit report.  
Each inquiry was listed in writing and identified by date and creditor. These inquiries are separate events and are treated independently under federal law.

Each fraudulent inquiry is a distinct item that the law requires TransUnion to block once identity theft is proven.

#### Step 2: How Many Times Identity Theft Was Properly Proven

Plaintiff did not submit identity theft documentation once.  
Plaintiff submitted **complete, legally sufficient identity theft documentation five separate times**, through multiple channels, including CFPB submissions and certified mail.

Each submission contained everything the law requires, including:
- An identity theft report,
- Proof of identity,
- Proof of address, and
- A written statement identifying the fraudulent inquiries.

Each time this documentation was received, federal law gave TransUnion **four business days** to block the fraudulent inquiries.

Each submission triggered a **new, independent legal obligation**.

#### Step 3: Why Multiple Violations Exist

Federal law does not allow TransUnion to ignore a submission simply because it has received one before.

Every time Plaintiff submitted proper documentation:
- TransUnion was required to act again,
- The four-business-day deadline restarted, and
- Each fraudulent inquiry had to be blocked.

TransUnion failed to block **any** of the fraudulent inquiries after **any** of the submissions.

As a result:
- There were **four fraudulent inquiries**, and
- **Five separate submissions** that triggered legal deadlines.

That means TransUnion missed **twenty separate mandatory obligations** to block known fraudulent information.

These are not repeated penalties for the same act.  
They are **separate failures to comply with separate legal duties**.

#### Step 4: Why This Method Is Conservative and Fair

This document does **not** count:
- Every day of delay,
- Every phone call,
- Every internal review,
- Or every emotional impact.

It counts only:
- Proven fraudulent items,

## SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (CLEAR AND TRACEABLE)

### 5.1 What Is Being Counted

Each count in this case represents a **separate legal duty imposed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act that TransUnion failed to perform**.

A violation is counted only when:
1. Plaintiff disputed information as identity theft,
2. Plaintiff submitted all documentation required by federal law, and
3. TransUnion failed to perform a mandatory statutory obligation after receiving that documentation.

No assumptions, estimates, or speculative multipliers are used.

---

### 5.2 Identity Theft Disputes and Submissions

Plaintiff disputed multiple items of information as identity theft and submitted **complete, legally sufficient identity theft documentation** on **multiple separate occasions**.

Each submission included everything required under FCRA §605B, including:
- An identity theft report,
- Proof of identity,
- Proof of address, and
- A written statement identifying the disputed information.

TransUnion **never identified any missing document**, **never claimed the submissions were incomplete**, and **affirmatively acknowledged receipt**.

---

### 5.3 Mandatory Legal Duty Under FCRA §605B

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, once a consumer submits the required identity theft documentation, a consumer reporting agency **must block the disputed information within four (4) business days**.

This duty:
- Is mandatory,
- Does not require an investigation,
- Does not require creditor permission,
- Does not allow additional documentation demands, and
- Does not allow delay based on internal policy or system limitations.

---

### 5.4 Failure to Perform the Mandatory §605B Block

Despite receiving complete and compliant identity theft submissions multiple times, TransUnion:
- Did not block the disputed information within four business days,
- Did not block it weeks later,
- Did not block it months later, and
- Continued reporting the disputed information.

Each time Plaintiff submitted a compliant identity theft packet, TransUnion incurred a **new, independent legal obligation** to block the disputed information.

Each time TransUnion failed to perform that obligation, it committed a **separate statutory violation**.

---

### 5.5 Refusal to Comply Despite Acknowledged Compliance

TransUnion’s responses did not state that Plaintiff’s submissions were deficient.

Instead, TransUnion:
- Acknowledged receipt,
- Affirmed that documentation was provided,
- Refused to perform the §605B block anyway,
- Stated the information would not be blocked,
- Told Plaintiff to “submit again,” and
- Refused to explain what was allegedly wrong or how to cure it.

Federal law does not allow a consumer reporting agency to:
- Reject a compliant §605B submission without explanation,
- Refuse to block information while admitting compliance, or
- Demand resubmission when nothing is missing.

Each such refusal constitutes an **independent statutory violation**.

---

### 5.6 Categories of Violations Counted

The violations in this case fall into the following categories, each tied to a separate statutory duty:

- Failure to block disputed identity theft information as required by §605B  
- Imposing documentation requirements not authorized by §605B  
- Refusing to provide lawful explanations for denials under §611  
- Refusing to explain how to cure an alleged deficiency under §611  
- Denying access to Plaintiff’s consumer file under §609  
- Continuing to report disputed information after identity theft notice under §1681e  
- Failing to conduct a reasonable investigation under §1681i  

Each category is counted separately because each involves a **different legal obligation**.

- TransUnion nonetheless refused to perform the required block, and
- TransUnion relied on internal policy or system limitations instead of federal law.

Choosing company policy over a mandatory federal statute is willful noncompliance as a matter of law.

- Ignoring repeated notice of identity theft,
- Refusing to act despite knowing the legal requirement,

- Refusing to explain or correct its position.

This satisfies the willfulness standard articulated in *Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr*.

- Allowed access to the consumer file, and
- Corrected known inaccurate reporting.

Each willful violation is subject to **statutory damages of up to $1,000 per violation** under 15 U.S.C. §1681n.

Each negligent violation is independently actionable under 15 U.S.C. §1681o.

### 6.2 §605B Failure-to-Block Counts (Primary Liability)

**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)

**Legal Duty:**  
Upon receipt of compliant identity theft documentation, TransUnion **must block the disputed information within four (4) business days**.

**Facts Establishing Liability:**
- Plaintiff disputed information as identity theft.
- Plaintiff submitted complete, legally sufficient documentation on **five separate occasions**.
- Each submission independently triggered a **new four-business-day statutory deadline**.
- TransUnion did not perform the required block after any submission.

**Count Rule:**

Five (5) separate §605B failures to block.

**Counts Assessed:**  
Counts 1–5

---

### 6.3 Unlawful Refusal to Perform §605B Despite Acknowledged Compliance

**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) and (c)

**Legal Duty:**  
If documentation is compliant, TransUnion **may not refuse to block**, demand resubmission, or impose extra conditions.

**Facts Establishing Liability:**
- TransUnion acknowledged receipt of Plaintiff’s submissions.
- TransUnion did not claim documentation was incomplete.

Five (5) unlawful refusals.

Counts 6–10

### 6.4 Illegal Documentation Demands and Conditions

**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2)

TransUnion demanded or relied on:
- Additional documentation not required by statute;
- Internal policy or system limitations;

One count for each distinct unlawful condition imposed in lieu of compliance.

Four (4) unlawful conditions imposed.

Counts 11–14

- Identify the specific deficiency; and

SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (SUMMARY)

5.1 Count Framework (How Counts Are Created)
Each “Count” below is a separate, independently actionable violation of a specific FCRA duty. A count is assessed only when all of the following occurred:

A. Plaintiff submitted a complete identity theft packet compliant with FCRA §605B (FTC Identity Theft Report, proof of identity, proof of address, and written statement identifying the disputed identity theft information); and  
B. TransUnion received or acknowledged the submission; and  
C. A mandatory statutory duty was triggered; and  
D. TransUnion failed to perform that duty, refused to perform it, or obstructed compliance by imposing conditions not authorized by law.

No “per day” inflation is used. Counts are event-based and duty-based.

5.2 Key Facts Used for Counting (From the Record)
A. Number of disputed identity theft inquiries/items at issue: 4  
B. Number of separate compliant §605B submissions made and received/acknowledged: 5  
C. TransUnion repeatedly refused §605B blocking, imposed policy/system excuses, and refused to provide legally sufficient denial explanations or cure instructions.

5.3 Count Ranges and Statutory Mapping (Total = 62)
Counts 1–25 — FCRA §605B violations (failure/refusal to block after compliant submissions)  
Counts 26–37 — Illegal documentation demands and unlawful conditions (FCRA §605B limitations)  
Counts 38–47 — Failure to provide lawful denial explanation and cure instructions (FCRA §611 / §1681i(a)(6))  
Counts 48–52 — Denial of access to the consumer file (FCRA §609 / §1681g)  
Counts 53–57 — Failure to maintain reasonable procedures to assure maximum possible accuracy (FCRA §1681e(b))  
Counts 58–62 — Failure to conduct a reasonable investigation / refusal to investigate after notice (FCRA §1681i)

Total Counts: 62

SECTION 6 — DETAILED COUNT BREAKDOWN WITH MECHANICAL MATH (TOTAL = 62)

6.0 Liability Standard (Willful, Reckless, Negligent)
TransUnion’s conduct constitutes, at minimum, negligent noncompliance under the FCRA. The record also supports willful noncompliance and reckless disregard because TransUnion received repeated notice of mandatory statutory duties, acknowledged receipt of compliant packets, substituted “company policy/system limitations” for federal law, refused to comply, and refused to explain or cure.

6.1 Counts 1–20 — Failure to Block Disputed Identity Theft Information After Compliant §605B Submissions
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)  
Duty: Block the disputed identity theft information within four (4) business days after receiving a compliant §605B submission.

Counting Rule (Mechanical):
One count is assessed for each disputed identity theft inquiry/item that remained unblocked after each compliant submission triggered a new four-business-day deadline.

Math:
4 disputed inquiries/items × 5 compliant submissions = 20 missed mandatory blocks

Counts Assessed:
Counts 1–20

What TransUnion is responsible for (Plain Language):
After each compliant submission, TransUnion had a mandatory duty to block each disputed identity theft inquiry/item. The failure to block after each triggered deadline is a separate violation.

6.2 Counts 21–25 — Refusal to Comply With §605B Despite Compliant Submissions (Policy/System Override and “Resubmit Again” Obstruction)
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) and §1681c-2(c)  
Duty: Comply with §605B upon receipt of compliant documentation and do not defeat the duty using internal policy, system limitations, or unauthorized prerequisites.

Counting Rule (Mechanical):
One count is assessed for each submission event where TransUnion refused to comply with §605B despite the packet being complete/compliant, including refusal statements such as: denying despite compliance, demanding re-submission without identifying a valid deficiency, or relying on “policy/system” to avoid blocking.

Math:
5 compliant submissions × 1 unlawful refusal/obstruction per submission = 5

Counts Assessed:
Counts 21–25

What TransUnion is responsible for (Plain Language):
Even when the packet was compliant, TransUnion refused to perform the mandatory block, used “policy/system limitations,” and instructed repeated re-submission instead of complying. Each refusal after a compliant submission is separately countable.

6.3 Counts 26–37 — Illegal Documentation Demands and Unlawful Conditions (Not Authorized by §605B)
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2)  
Duty: Do not require documents or conditions beyond what §605B allows.

Count Basis:
TransUnion demanded or conditioned compliance on items and constraints not authorized by statute (including creditor letters, police reports when an FTC report was provided, “one packet per inquiry” rules, internal system limitations, or other extra-statutory prerequisites).

Counting Rule:
One count per discrete unlawful demand/condition instance documented across the record.

Counts Assessed:
Counts 26–37 (12 counts)

What TransUnion is responsible for:
TransUnion cannot replace federal law with internal requirements. Each extra-statutory condition imposed instead of compliance is a separate violation.

6.4 Counts 38–47 — Failure to Provide Lawful Denial Explanation and Cure Instructions
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii) (commonly pled as §611 duties)  
Duty: If denying/refusing relief, TransUnion must provide a legally sufficient explanation and the information necessary to cure, not vague statements.

Count Basis:
TransUnion issued denials such as “documents not acceptable” while refusing to (a) identify what was missing, (b) explain how to fix it, or (c) cite any lawful basis—despite repeated submissions and requests for clarification.

Counting Rule:
One count per defective denial/response lacking lawful explanation and cure information.

Counts Assessed:
Counts 38–47 (10 counts)

What TransUnion is responsible for:
If TransUnion claims noncompliance, it must specify the deficiency and how to cure. Refusing to do so is separately actionable.

6.5 Counts 48–52 — Denial of Access to Consumer File
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681g (often pled as §609 file disclosure/access duties)  
Duty: Provide file access/disclosure upon proper identification; do not use lockout as a mechanism to deny statutory rights.

Count Basis:
Plaintiff was locked out of the TransUnion account/file and access was not restored despite identity verification, and the lockout was used to obstruct §605B rights.

Counting Rule:
One count per distinct access denial/lockout refusal event documented.

Counts Assessed:
Counts 48–52 (5 counts)

What TransUnion is responsible for:
TransUnion cannot deny file access and then use that denial to block the consumer from enforcing §605B rights.

6.6 Counts 53–57 — Failure to Maintain Reasonable Procedures to Assure Maximum Possible Accuracy
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681e(b)  
Duty: Maintain reasonable procedures to assure maximum possible accuracy and promptly correct known identity theft inaccuracies.

Count Basis:
Despite repeated identity theft notice and compliant documentation, TransUnion continued to report the disputed identity theft information.

Counting Rule:
One count per documented accuracy failure event after notice.

Counts Assessed:
Counts 53–57 (5 counts)

What TransUnion is responsible for:
Continuing to publish known-disputed identity theft information after repeated notice supports negligence at minimum and willfulness/recklessness where policy-driven.

6.7 Counts 58–62 — Failure/Refusal to Conduct a Reasonable Investigation After Notice
Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681i  
Duty: Conduct a reasonable investigation upon dispute/notice and do not refuse investigation as a substitute for compliance.

Count Basis:
TransUnion admitted (including on recorded calls) that no investigation occurred and maintained a refusal posture instead of investigating and correcting.

Counting Rule:
One count per documented failure/refusal to investigate event after notice.

Counts Assessed:
Counts 58–62 (5 counts)

What TransUnion is responsible for:
Refusing to investigate or admitting no investigation after notice is separately actionable under §1681i.

6.8 Total Count Tally (Judge-Tally Ready)
Counts 1–20: 20  
Counts 21–25: 5  
Counts 26–37: 12  
Counts 38–47: 10  
Counts 48–52: 5  
Counts 53–57: 5  
Counts 58–62: 5  
Total: 62

5.3 Mandatory Legal Duty Under FCRA §605B
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, once a consumer submits the required identity theft documentation, a consumer reporting agency must block the disputed information within four (4) business days.

Is mandatory,
Does not require an investigation,
Does not require creditor permission,
Does not allow additional documentation demands, and
Does not allow delay based on internal policy or system limitations.

Federal law does not allow a consumer reporting agency to:
Reject a compliant §605B submission without explanation,
Refuse to block information while admitting compliance, or
Demand resubmission when nothing is missing.
Each such refusal constitutes an independent statutory violation.

Failure to block disputed identity theft information as required by §605B
Imposing documentation requirements not authorized by §605B
Refusing to provide lawful explanations for denials under §611

## SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (SUMMARY)

### 5.1 How Violations Are Counted in This Case

The violations in this matter are **not speculative, cumulative, or item-based**.  
They are **event-based and duty-based**, tied directly to mandatory obligations imposed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act.

A violation is counted **only when all three of the following occurred**:

1. Plaintiff disputed information as identity theft  
2. Plaintiff submitted all documentation required by FCRA §605B  
3. TransUnion failed to perform a mandatory statutory duty after receipt  

No violation is counted based on:
- assumptions,
- estimates,
- daily multipliers, or
- the number of disputed accounts or inquiries.

Each count corresponds to a **distinct legal duty that TransUnion failed to perform after notice**.

---

### 5.2 Core Duty Triggering Most Violations — FCRA §605B

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, once a consumer submits a compliant identity theft packet, a consumer reporting agency **must block the disputed information within four (4) business days**.

This duty:
- is mandatory,
- does not require investigation,
- does not require creditor permission,
- does not permit additional documentation demands, and
- does not permit delay based on internal policy or system limitations.

Each time TransUnion received a **complete §605B submission**, it incurred a **new, independent statutory obligation** to block the disputed information.

Each time TransUnion failed to do so, a **separate violation occurred**.

---

### 5.3 Summary of Counted Violations

The following counts are based on **documented submissions, responses, and refusals**, not assumptions:

- **Counts 1–25**  
  Failure to perform the mandatory §605B block after receiving compliant identity theft documentation  

- **Counts 26–37**  
  Imposing documentation requirements not authorized by §605B after acknowledging receipt of compliant submissions  

- **Counts 38–47**  
  Refusing to provide lawful denial explanations or instructions on how to cure under §611  

- **Counts 48–52**  
  Denying Plaintiff access to his consumer file in violation of §609  

- **Counts 53–57**  
  Continuing to report information known to be fraudulent after identity theft notice, violating §1681e  

- **Counts 58–62**  
  Admitting that no investigation occurred despite statutory duties under §1681i  

**Total Counted Violations: 62**

Each count reflects a **separate statutory breach**, supported by notice, opportunity to comply, and failure.
