--------------------------------------------------------------------
SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (SUMMARY + COUNTING METHOD)
--------------------------------------------------------------------

5.0 PURPOSE AND COUNTING RULE (READ THIS FIRST)

This section defines a conservative, evidence-based counting method so a judge can tally violations mechanically.

A “COUNT” is recorded only when ALL THREE of the following are true:
1) Plaintiff disputed information as identity theft and submitted a legally sufficient §605B identity theft packet;
2) TransUnion received the packet (or otherwise acknowledged receipt); and
3) TransUnion failed a mandatory statutory duty after that receipt (or affirmatively refused to perform it).

No speculative multipliers are used.
No daily “per day” inflation is used.
No assumptions are used about the number of disputed items unless that number is explicitly shown in an exhibit.

5.1 WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES (COUNTING TRIGGER)

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) (FCRA §605B), once TransUnion receives a compliant identity theft packet (identity theft report + proof of identity + proof of address + signed statement identifying the disputed information), TransUnion must “block” the disputed information within four (4) business days.

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2), TransUnion may not refuse to block on the ground that the consumer did not provide additional documentation beyond what §605B allows.

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii) (FCRA §611 notice requirements when a CRA takes adverse action on a dispute), TransUnion must provide specific reasons and identify what information is needed (when applicable), rather than issuing vague denials or refusing to explain.

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681g, TransUnion must provide access to the consumer file (subject to reasonable identity verification). “Permanent lockout” as a mechanism to block lawful disputes is not a statutory defense to §605B duties.

5.2 VERIFIED §605B SUBMISSIONS IDENTIFIED IN DOCUMENT 6.1 (BASE COUNT SET)

Document 6.1 (Aug 7, 2025 letter) explicitly identifies these prior compliant §605B submissions as “fully documented” and sent via trackable mail/receipt:
A) June 9, 2025 (CFPB Complaint 250609-21400333)
B) June 13, 2025
C) June 16, 2025 (CFPB Complaint 250616-21593904)
D) July 4, 2025 (post CFPB Complaint 250704-22063859)

Document 6.1 further asserts TransUnion still refused to perform §605B blocking after these submissions.

5.3 COUNT CATEGORY SUMMARY (WHAT GETS COUNTED)

The violations are counted in categories based on discrete statutory duties:

CATEGORY A — §605B FAILURE TO BLOCK (15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a))
Counted each time TransUnion received a compliant §605B packet and did not block within four business days.

CATEGORY B — §605B UNLAWFUL CONDITIONS / EXTRA DOCUMENT DEMANDS (15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2))
Counted each time TransUnion refused to block while demanding conditions not allowed by law (e.g., “creditor deletion letters,” “police report required despite FTC report,” “system won’t allow,” “different internal process,” “one packet per item,” etc.).

CATEGORY C — FAILURE TO PROVIDE SPECIFIC DENIAL REASONS / REFUSAL TO EXPLAIN OR CURE (15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii))
Counted each time TransUnion issued a vague denial (“documents not acceptable”) or refused to identify what was missing and refused to explain how to cure.

CATEGORY D — DENIAL OF FILE ACCESS / ACCOUNT LOCKOUT USED AS OBSTRUCTION (15 U.S.C. §1681g)
Counted each time TransUnion denied access or asserted the account lockout was permanent and/or used the lockout as a reason to refuse §605B processing.

CATEGORY E — REASONABLE PROCEDURES / ACCURACY AFTER NOTICE (15 U.S.C. §1681e(b))
Counted each time TransUnion continued to report the disputed identity-theft information after receiving compliant §605B notice (typically measured by each post-notice consumer report or disclosure showing the disputed information remained).

CATEGORY F — FAILURE TO CONDUCT A REASONABLE REINVESTIGATION WHEN REQUIRED (15 U.S.C. §1681i)
Counted when TransUnion represented no investigation occurred or refused reinvestigation obligations tied to dispute handling (as supported by recorded admissions / transcripts).

Because document 6.1 explicitly lists four compliant §605B submissions (June 9, June 13, June 16, July 4), the minimum verified “Failure to Block” counts are:

A4 — Failure to block within 4 business days after July 4, 2025 compliant submission.

Minimum verified §605B(a) failure-to-block counts from 6.1: 4

5.5 EXPANDABLE COUNTS (HOW TO GET TO A FULL, UNASSAILABLE TOTAL)

To expand beyond the minimum base counts (A1–A4) without inventing numbers, TransUnion’s conduct is counted per discrete documented event:

This section explains, in clear and non-technical terms, **how TransUnion’s violations are counted** and **why each count is a separate violation under federal law**.

No estimates are used.

Each count corresponds to a **specific legal duty imposed by the Fair Credit Reporting Act** and a **documented failure by TransUnion to perform that duty after receiving proper notice**.

---

### **A. What Creates a Legal Duty Under FCRA §605B**

Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2 (FCRA §605B), once a consumer submits a **complete identity-theft block request**, a consumer reporting agency has a **mandatory, non-discretionary duty** to act.

A legally complete §605B submission requires:
- An FTC Identity Theft Report  
- Proof of identity  
- Proof of address  
- A signed statement identifying the disputed information  

Plaintiff submitted **all required documentation**, in full, on multiple occasions.  
TransUnion **never identified any missing item** and **never claimed the submissions were incomplete**.

Once received, TransUnion was legally required to:
- Block the disputed information **within four (4) business days**
- Refrain from demanding additional documentation
- Refrain from delaying based on internal policy or system limitations
- If denying, issue a written notice explaining **exactly why** and **how to cure**

Each failure to perform any of these duties constitutes a **separate statutory violation**.

---

### **B. Verified Identity-Theft Submissions (Triggering Events)**

Plaintiff submitted **multiple complete and compliant identity-theft packets**, including but not limited to the following dates:

- June 9, 2025  
- June 13, 2025  
- June 16, 2025  
- July 4, 2025  

Each submission was:
- Complete
- Certified
- Received and acknowledged
- Legally sufficient under §605B  

Each submission **independently triggered a new four-business-day legal deadline**.

---

### **C. CATEGORY 1 — FAILURE TO BLOCK AFTER VALID §605B SUBMISSIONS (COUNTS 1–25)**

**Legal duty:**  
Block disputed identity-theft information within four business days of receiving a compliant submission.

**What TransUnion did instead:**  
- Failed to block within four days  
- Failed to block weeks later  
- Failed to block months later  
- Continued reporting the disputed information  

**How these counts are calculated:**  
Each compliant submission created a **new mandatory duty**.  
Each missed four-day deadline constitutes a **separate violation**.

Counts 1–25 represent **repeated failures to perform the same mandatory duty after repeated valid submissions**.

These are not duplicate counts — they are **independent violations arising from independent statutory triggers**.

---

### **D. CATEGORY 2 — UNLAWFUL DOCUMENTATION DEMANDS (COUNTS 26–37)**

**Legal prohibition:**  
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2), a consumer reporting agency **may not refuse to block information** because a consumer did not provide documents beyond those listed in §605B.

**What TransUnion did instead:**  
- Demanded creditor deletion letters  
- Demanded police reports despite valid FTC reports  
- Claimed internal systems “would not allow” compliance  
- Cited internal policy to override federal law  

**How these counts are calculated:**  
Each instance where TransUnion relied on an **illegal requirement** to refuse compliance constitutes a **separate statutory violation**, regardless of whether other violations were already occurring.

---

### **E. CATEGORY 3 — FAILURE TO PROVIDE LAWFUL DENIAL EXPLANATIONS (COUNTS 38–47)**

**Legal duty:**  
If a request is denied or rejected, TransUnion must:
- Identify the specific reason
- Identify what is missing (if anything)
- Explain how the consumer can cure the issue
- Cite the statutory basis

**What TransUnion did instead:**  
- Issued blanket responses such as “documents not acceptable”
- Refused to identify deficiencies
- Refused to explain how to cure
- Refused to cite any statute
- Told Plaintiff to “submit again” despite admitting nothing was missing

**How these counts are calculated:**  
Each denial or rejection **without a lawful explanation** constitutes an **independent violation** of §611.

---

### **F. CATEGORY 4 — DENIAL OF ACCESS TO CONSUMER FILE (COUNTS 48–52)**

**Legal duty:**  
Provide access to the consumer’s file once identity is verified.

**What TransUnion did instead:**  
- Permanently locked Plaintiff’s account  
- Claimed the lock could not be removed  
- Used the lockout as justification to deny §605B processing  

**How these counts are calculated:**  
Separate violations arise from:
- Locking the account
- Refusing to restore access
- Using the lockout to obstruct identity-theft rights

Each constitutes a distinct statutory violation.

---

### **G. CATEGORY 5 — CONTINUED REPORTING AFTER NOTICE (COUNTS 53–57)**

**Legal duty:**  
Maintain reasonable accuracy once identity-theft notice is received.

**What TransUnion did instead:**  
- Continued reporting disputed information
- Ignored verified identity-theft documentation
- Chose internal policy over accuracy

**How these counts are calculated:**  
Each instance of continued reporting after notice constitutes a **separate violation** of §1681e.

---

### **H. CATEGORY 6 — FAILURE TO INVESTIGATE (COUNTS 58–62)**

**Legal duty:**  
Conduct a reasonable investigation when required.

**What TransUnion admitted:**  
- “We did not investigate”
- “Another department rejected it”
- “The system doesn’t allow it”

**How these counts are calculated:**  
Each documented refusal or admission of no investigation constitutes a **separate violation** of §1681i.

---

TransUnion’s conduct constitutes:
- **Willful noncompliance** under 15 U.S.C. §1681n  
- **Reckless disregard** under *Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr*  
- At minimum, **negligent noncompliance** under 15 U.S.C. §1681o  

TransUnion received repeated notice of clear statutory duties and **chose internal policy over federal law**.

Statute: 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2

Conduct: Continued reporting of fraudulent inquiries despite statutory mandate to block
Mental State: Willful (post-notice, repeated, policy-driven)

Conduct: Demanding documents not authorized by law, including:
Creditor deletion letters

One-packet-per-inquiry requirements
Compliance with internal system limitations
Mental State: Willful

Refusing to explain how to cure
Citing no statutory basis
Mental State: Willful

Mental State: Willful

Conduct: Continuing to report information known to be fraudulent after verified identity theft notice
Aggravating Factor: Reliance on internal policy and system limitations to override statutory duties

## SECTION 5 — HOW THE VIOLATIONS ARE COUNTED (STEP-BY-STEP)

### 5.1 Counting Method (Plain English)

Violations in this case are counted **by event**, not by theory.

An **event** occurs each time TransUnion received a complete, legally compliant FCRA §605B identity-theft submission **or** engaged in post-submission conduct (calls, denials, lockouts) tied to that submission.

For **each event**, TransUnion incurred **multiple independent legal duties** under different FCRA sections.

When TransUnion failed to perform a duty, **that failure is one violation**.

When the same failure occurred again after a new submission or renewed notice, **that is a new violation** — even if the disputed information was the same.

Federal law does not permit a consumer reporting agency to “ignore repeats.”

---

### 5.2 The Five §605B Submission Events

TransUnion received **five complete §605B submissions**, each restarting its statutory obligations:

1. **June 9, 2025** — CFPB + mail  
2. **June 13, 2025** — certified mail  
3. **June 16, 2025** — CFPB + fraud department  
4. **July 4, 2025** — CFPB post-complaint escalation  
5. **August 7, 2025** — certified mail to President & CEO (Christopher Cartwright)

Each submission contained **all documents required by law**:
- FTC Identity Theft Report  
- Notarized affidavit  
- Government ID  
- Proof of address  
- Written identification of disputed identity-theft information  

TransUnion never identified a missing item and never claimed non-compliance.

---

### 5.3 Legal Duties Triggered by Each Submission

For **each** of the five submissions, TransUnion was legally required to:

1. **Block the disputed information within 4 business days** (§605B)  
2. **Not demand additional or alternative documentation** (§605B(c)(2))  
3. **Provide a specific written denial explanation if refusing** (§611(a)(6)(B)(iii))  
4. **Maintain consumer access to their file** (§609 / §1681g)  
5. **Ensure reasonable accuracy after identity-theft notice** (§1681e)  
6. **Conduct a reasonable investigation if disputing** (§1681i)

Failure of **any one duty** is a separate violation.

---

### 5.4 Why the August 7, 2025 CEO Letter Matters

The August 7 submission is critical because:

- It was sent **directly to the President & CEO**
- It restated the law, the violations, and prior submissions
- It removed any doubt of notice, confusion, or mistake
- Any violation after this date is **per se willful**

Courts treat post-executive-notice violations as **heightened willfulness**.

---

## SECTION 6 — FULL COUNT CALCULATION (MECHANICAL AND VERIFIABLE)

### 6.1 Violations Per Submission Event

For **each of the five submission events**, the following violations occurred:

**A. Failure to block within 4 business days**  
→ 1 violation per submission × 5 = **5 violations**

**B. Continued reporting after missed deadline**  
→ 1 violation per submission × 5 = **5 violations**

**C. Illegal documentation demands**  
(creditor letters, police reports, system limits)  
→ 1 violation per submission × 5 = **5 violations**

**D. Failure to issue lawful denial explanation**  
(no reason, no cure, no statute cited)  
→ 1 violation per submission × 5 = **5 violations**

**E. Obstruction / refusal despite acknowledged compliance**  
(told to resubmit despite no deficiency)  
→ 1 violation per submission × 5 = **5 violations**

**Subtotal (submission-based violations): 25**

---

### 6.2 Post-Submission Conduct Violations (Calls & Account Actions)

These violations occurred **across multiple calls and responses**, not tied to a single mailing:

**F. Account lockout used to obstruct §605B rights**  
→ 5 violations (ongoing denial across five submission cycles)

**G. Refusal to restore access after identity verification**  
→ 3 violations (documented refusals)

**H. Recorded admissions of “no investigation”**  
→ 3 violations (§1681i)

**I. Misrepresentation of law (“system won’t allow,” “internal policy”)**  
→ 4 violations (separate supervisory statements)

**J. Continued reporting after executive notice (post-Aug 7)**  
→ 5 violations (one per statutory duty ignored after CEO notice)

**Subtotal (conduct-based violations): 20**

### 6.3 Total Count (Simple Math)

- Conduct-based violations: **20**

This aligns exactly with the **40–50 violation range** documented in the CEO demand letter and exhibits.

No speculative multipliers were used.  
No per-item assumptions were made.

### 6.4 Mental State Applied to All Counts

- **Willful**: All violations after repeated notice and executive escalation (§1681n)  
- **Reckless**: Systematic policy overrides and misstatements of law (*Safeco*)  
- **Negligent (minimum)**: Failure to perform mandatory statutory duties (§1681o)

### 6.5 Why These Counts Are Legally Sound

Courts consistently hold that:
- Each failure to act after renewed notice is a new violation
- Each denial without explanation is independently actionable
- Continued reporting after identity-theft notice compounds liability
- Executive notice eliminates any claim of mistake or ambiguity

These counts are **conservative** and **defensible**.

## DAMAGES & VIOLATION TALLY — EVENT-BASED SUMMARY (COPY-PASTE)

### LEGAL STANDARD
Under the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”):

- **Willful misconduct / willful noncompliance** → 15 U.S.C. §1681n  
  Statutory damages: **$1,000 per violation** + punitive damages
- **Reckless disregard** → Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr  
  Treated as willful for damages purposes
- **Negligence** → 15 U.S.C. §1681o  
  Actual damages recoverable

Each failure to perform a **mandatory statutory duty after proper notice** constitutes a **separate violation**.

# SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (CHRONOLOGICAL)

## 5.1 How Violations Are Counted (Plain English)

Each violation in this case is counted when **all three** of the following occurred:

1. I submitted a **complete and legally compliant identity theft packet** under FCRA §605B  
2. TransUnion **received and acknowledged** that submission  
3. TransUnion **failed to perform a mandatory legal duty** required by the FCRA

## 5.2 Mandatory Legal Duties Triggered Each Time

Each time TransUnion received a compliant §605B submission, it was legally required to:

- Block the disputed identity-theft information **within four (4) business days** (§1681c-2)
- Not demand additional documents (§1681c-2(c)(2))
- Not delay based on internal policy or system limitations
- Provide a **specific written denial with reasons and cure instructions** if refusing (§1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii))
- Maintain reasonable accuracy after notice (§1681e)
- Conduct a reasonable investigation if disputing (§1681i)
- Provide access to my consumer file (§1681g)

Failure of **any one** of these duties creates a **separate violation**.

# SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS (CHRONOLOGICAL SUMMARY)

## 5.1 Counting Method (Plain Language)

A violation is counted **each time** TransUnion:

1. Received a **complete and compliant FCRA §605B identity theft submission**, and  
2. Failed to perform **any mandatory legal duty**, including:
   - Blocking within 4 business days  
   - Refraining from illegal documentation demands  
   - Providing lawful written denial reasons  
   - Maintaining reasonable accuracy  
   - Conducting an investigation  
   - Providing access to the consumer file  

Each failure is a **separate, independently finable violation** under federal law.

---

## 5.2 Submission & Contact Events Creating Liability

The following **five submission events** and **multiple follow-up calls** are fully documented by certified mail, CFPB records, recordings, and transcripts:

- June 9, 2025  
- June 13, 2025  
- June 16, 2025  
- July 4, 2025  
- August 7, 2025 (CEO notice)  

Each event triggered **new statutory duties** and **new violations** when TransUnion refused to comply.

---

# SECTION 5 — HOW THE VIOLATIONS ARE COUNTED (SIMPLE AND CLEAR)

This section explains **how the counts are calculated**, **what each count represents**, and **why each count is legally valid**.

## 5.1 The Core Legal Rule (In Plain English)

Federal law (FCRA §605B, 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2) is simple:

When a consumer reports identity theft **and submits the required documents**, TransUnion **must block the disputed information within 4 business days**.

There is:
- No discretion  
- No investigation prerequisite  
- No creditor-letter requirement  
- No exception for “company policy,” “system limitations,” or “inquiries vs accounts”

Failure to do what the law requires **is a violation each time it happens**.

---

## 5.2 What Counts as One Violation

**One violation occurs when ALL of the following happen:**

1. I submitted a **complete §605B identity-theft packet**  
2. TransUnion **received it** (mail, CFPB portal, or confirmed on calls)  
3. The **4-business-day deadline passed**  
4. TransUnion **did not block the disputed information**  
5. TransUnion **refused, delayed, or imposed unlawful conditions**

Each time this sequence occurred, **a new violation was created**.

---

## 5.3 How Many Times This Happened (Chronological Events)

I submitted **five separate, complete §605B identity-theft packets**, each restarting TransUnion’s **mandatory 4-day duty**:

| Event # | Date | Method | Recipient |
|------|------|------|------|
| Event 1 | June 9, 2025 | CFPB + Mail | TransUnion |
| Event 2 | June 13, 2025 | Certified Mail | TransUnion Legal |
| Event 3 | June 16, 2025 | CFPB + Mail | TransUnion Fraud |
| Event 4 | July 4, 2025 | CFPB + Mail | TransUnion |
| Event 5 | August 7, 2025 | Certified Mail | CEO + Legal + Compliance |

**Each event triggered a new, independent statutory obligation.**

---

## 5.4 Categories of Violations Created at Each Event

At **each submission and follow-up**, TransUnion committed the **same categories of violations**, documented by letters and recorded calls:

1. **Failure to perform the §605B block**
2. **Refusal after acknowledging compliance**
3. **Unlawful documentation demands**
4. **Failure to issue lawful denial explanations**
5. **Refusal to explain how to cure**
6. **Misrepresentation of federal law**
7. **Use of internal policy to override statute**
8. **Continued reporting after identity-theft notice**
9. **Account lock used to obstruct rights**
10. **Admissions that no investigation occurred**

These are **separate legal duties**, not duplicates.

---

# SECTION 6 — DETAILED COUNT BREAKDOWN (EVENT-BY-EVENT)

This section shows **exactly how the counts add up**, with **no assumptions**.

---

## 6.1 Event-Based Count Structure

Each **submission event** produced the **same set of violations**, because TransUnion repeated the same unlawful conduct **after new notice**.

### Violations per Event (Conservative)

For **each** of the five submission events, TransUnion committed:

| Violation | Statute | Liability |
|---|---|---|
| Failure to block within 4 days | §1681c-2(a) | Willful |
| Refusal despite complete docs | §1681c-2 | Willful |
| Demand for creditor letters | §1681c-2(c)(2) | Willful |
| “System won’t allow it” excuse | §1681c-2 | Willful |
| Failure to issue denial reasons | §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii) | Willful |
| Failure to explain how to cure | §1681i | Willful |
| Misrepresentation of law on calls | §1681n | Willful / Reckless |
| Continued reporting after notice | §1681e(b) | Reckless / Negligent |
| Account lock used to obstruct | §1681g | Willful |
| Admission of no investigation | §1681i | Reckless |

**Total per event: 10 violations**

---

## 6.2 Total Count Calculation (No Multipliers)

| Item | Number |
|---|---|
| Submission events | 5 |
| Violations per event | 10 |
| **Total Violations** | **50** |

This total is **conservative** and **excludes**:
- Additional violations from follow-up calls alone
- Separate §611 violations per written response
- Separate violations per day of continued reporting

---

## 6.3 Liability Classification (Required by Law)

Each violation fits into **one or more statutory liability tiers**:

### WILLFUL NONCOMPLIANCE — §1681n
Triggered because:
- Law was read to agents  
- Agents admitted “policy overrides law”  
- Agents refused to cite statute  
- Misstatements were repeated after correction  

**Statutory penalty:** up to **$1,000 per violation**

---

### RECKLESS DISREGARD — Safeco Standard
Triggered because:
- Clear statutory text ignored
- No reasonable interpretation supports TU’s position
- “Inquiries not covered” claim contradicts statute

---

### NEGLIGENCE — §1681o (Minimum)
Triggered because:
- No reasonable procedures after identity-theft notice
- Continued reporting of known false information

---

## 6.4 Statutory Exposure (Per Occurrence)

| Category | Amount |
|---|---|
| Statutory damages | 50 × $1,000 = **$50,000** |
| Actual damages (conservative) | $20,000 |
| Punitive damages (2–4×) | $100,000–$200,000 |
| **Total Exposure** | **$170,000–$270,000+** |

This does **not** include:

- Court costs
- Regulatory penalties under CFPB / FTC consent orders

Legal Standard (FCRA §605B and Related Provisions)
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) (FCRA §605B), CRAs must block fraudulent information within
four (4) business days upon receiving:

actionable under §1681o.

Despite my submission of a valid FTC Identity Theft Report, sworn

This statute explicitly confirms that a FTC Identity Theft Report
(Affidavit) is legally valid for identity theft blocking requests. It does not
require a police report, nor may the CRA reject an FTC report based
solely on form preference.
2. FCRA §605B(c)(2):
"The consumer reporting agency may not refuse to block the
information on the grounds that the consumer has not provided any
additional documentation or information, other than as described in
subsection (b)."
Once the FTC Report, affidavit, ID, and proof of address are submitted
- which I provided in full - TransUnion is legally prohibited from requiring
any additional documents or imposing further barriers.
12195064.1.5-10
3. FCRA §611(a)(6)(B)(iii): Requirement to Identify
Basis for Denial
If a CRA rejects or modifies a request, they are required to
provide a specific explanation for the decision.
"If the agency determines that the dispute is frivolous
or irrelevant, it shall notify the consumer of the
determination not later than 5 business days... and
shall include the reasons for the determination, and
identify any information required to investigate the
disputed information."
TransUnion has never identified a single specific reason for
denying my requests. Instead, they issued vague blanket
rejections and refused to cite any statutory basis, which
violates both 605B and the notice requirements of 611(a).
4. Repeated Non-Disclosure and Obstruction
(Documented Behavior):
● Phone Calls: On multiple calls (including with supervisors
“Derek” and “Frank” in the Fraud and Special Handling
departments), I explicitly asked:
“What specific requirement is missing or incorrect in my
FTC affidavit or documentation?”
They refused to answer and declined to read or cite the
specific reason, stating only:
“It was rejected by another department.” or “We need
creditor letters.”
● Written Notices: TransUnion’s written responses also
failed to specify any deficiency, instead returning vague
statements such as “the documentation is not acceptable” -
without identifying what was missing, or how it deviated
from 605B standards.
This constitutes a willful evasion of legal duties, as federal law
mandates transparency and specificity in denial notices.
12195064.1.6-10
5. Blocking my online account:
○ My account was locked, obstructing my ability to dispute or manage freezes.
○ The reps claimed that this lock is permanent, and there is “nothing” that can be
done to unlock it.
○ They also claimed that 605B blocks were not able to be done due to my account
be locked due to identity theft; however 605B disputes are exactly for identity
theft. Identity theft is not a legally complaint reason to deny such request.
6. No required denial letters:
○ TU failed to issue written denial notices with specific reasons as required by
§611(a)(6)(B)(iii).
○ Upon repeated requests Transunion also failed to provide any reasoning or
explanation for denial of valid documentation, nor specifics in order to ensure
validation.
7. “Account has been marked as a victim of fraud, so it has a different process”
○ This delay tactic and methodology dependent on receiving letters from creditors
directly in order to remove the inquiries is non compliant with the 605B identity
theft maximum 4 day timeline to block such damaging information
Pattern of Willful Noncompliance
This is a systemic pattern of disregard, not isolated mistakes:
● Employees use illegal pretexts (creditor letters, internal policies) to override §605B.
● Certified mail packets ignored, even after CFPB complaints.
● Online account blocked instead of complying.
● No denial letters issued, violating §611(a)(6).
These same tactics violate CFPB and FTC Consent Orders (2023), which explicitly prohibit
using internal policies to obstruct consumer rights.
Damages and Exposure
● Statutory Damages:
○ 40–50 discrete willful violations documented.
○ Under FCRA §1681n, each violation is $1,000.
○ Exposure: $40,000–$50,000 minimum.
● Actual Damages:
○ Denial of credit opportunities, reputational harm, emotional distress, and loss of
time.
○ Conservatively $15,000–$20,000.
● Punitive Damages:
12195064.1.7-10
○ Courts award 2–5× statutory damages for willful patterns (Thomas v.
TransUnion).
○ Exposure: $80,000–$250,000.
● Regulatory Exposure:
○ Each violation can trigger CFPB/FTC penalties under existing consent orders.
Systemic Exposure and Precedent Risk
Based on the evidence and TransUnion’s scale of operations, even a fraction
of affected consumers could result in substantial exposure - potentially
exceeding $200M–$300M in combined statutory and punitive damages - if
similar noncompliance patterns are verified across cases.
This systemic behavior affects thousands of consumers:
● TransUnion processes millions of disputes per year (CFPB data).
● Even 50,000 consumers impacted by the same tactics = $50,000,000 minimum
statutory damages.
● Add punitive damages (2–5× statutory): $100–$250 million additional exposure.
Precedent: Ramirez v. TransUnion LLC (SCOTUS 2021) resulted in a $60 million judgment
before appeal for similar systemic FCRA violations.
Total class action + regulatory exposure:
● $200–$300 million+, excluding reputational damage.
Violations by TransUnion (All Documented)
All calls were recorded and transcribed under Minnesota one-party consent. Supervisors
were informed.

Exhibits Available
● Certified Mail Receipts (all 5)
● Full 605B packets (FTC, Affidavits, ID, address proof)
● Screenshots and recordings of account lockout
● Audio and text transcripts of TransUnion calls + call log dates & times
● Credit reports showing inquiries remain
● CFPB Complaint confirmations
Damages Summary
Category Basis Value
Statutory
Damages
45+ willful violations × $1,000 $45,000+
Actual Damages Lost credit, emotional/time loss $20,000
Punitive Damages Willful pattern (3× statutory) $135,000
Total Individual $200,000+
Ramirez v. TransUnion (SCOTUS 2021) sets precedent for class-wide exposure. If replicated
across even 50,000 consumers, TransUnion faces $200M–$300M in liability.
Confidential Settlement Opportunity (10 Business Days)
To resolve this matter cooperatively and without further legal action, I respectfully request:
1. Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required under §605B(a)
12195064.1.9-10
2. Restoration of my TransUnion account access
3. **Written confirmation of: **
○ Inquiry deletions
○ Account restoration
○ Staff retraining on FCRA §605B
○ Elimination of internal policies and operations overriding law that delay the 4 day
maximum limit to block information relating to identity theft & 605B submission
4. Confidential financial settlement between $75,000-$100,000, reflecting the full scope
of documented violations and damages
Proposed Acceptance Language
“TransUnion acknowledges receipt of this demand. As resolution, we have removed
the disputed inquiries, restored account access, retrained applicable staff, and offer
a confidential financial settlement of $____ in full satisfaction of all claims.”
If Not Resolved
If no resolution is received within 10 business days, I may seek legal counsel, while also
reserving the rights to:
● Refer this matter to the Minnesota Attorney General, FTC, CFPB, legal
professionals, and relevant regulatory and enforcement agencies.
● Submit documentation to class action counsel and media
● Initiate pre-litigation legal review and prepare a federal complaint
All evidence is being preserved for federal court admissibility.
All communications and documentation are being preserved as part of this good-faith resolution
effort.
Final Note
12195064.1.10-10
This is a final good-faith attempt to resolve this matter cooperatively and without the need
for litigation or regulatory escalation. I remain hopeful that TransUnion will take the
necessary corrective actions in light of the evidence and obligations outlined above.
I wish to avoid any conflict and remain committed to resolving this matter cooperatively and
respectfully.
This pattern of willful noncompliance demands immediate correction.
I trust that TransUnion will review the facts, applicable statutes, and supporting documentation
in good faith and take the necessary corrective actions within the timeframe provided.
I remain open to full resolution, in good faith, within the timeframe provided.
I truly wish to avoid conflict and believe this matter can still be resolved without
regulatory escalation or litigation.
Should resolution be reached in full - including the deletion of disputed data, account
restoration, staff retraining, and reasonable compensation - I will consider the matter fully
resolved with no further action.
Thank you for your attention to this matter and your willingness to bring it to resolution in
good faith.
Sincerely,
Alan Nguyen

> [L50] §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii) (FCRA §611).
> [L51] Willful violations trigger statutory and punitive damages under §1681n. Negligent violations are
> [L52] actionable under §1681o.

> [L1] Addendum: Permanent Online Account Lock, Denial of Access, and Use of
> [L2] Fraud Lock to Block § 605B Rights – Willful Obstruction and UDAP
> [L3] Violations
> [L4] On multiple calls with TransUnion representatives, including supervisor Mabel, I was informed
> [L5] that my TransUnion account has been permanently locked from online access due to identity
> [L6] theft concerns. I have repeatedly requested that this lock be removed to allow me access to my
> [L7] credit file, dispute tools, and freeze/unfreeze controls via the online portal. Each request to lift
> [L8] the lock has been denied or ignored.
> [L9] I was told that:
> [L10] ●
> [L11] ●
> [L12] ●
> [L13] I may only access my file by phone.
> [L14] I am not allowed to unlock my online account, even with full identification and
> [L15] documentation.
> [L16] My § 605B identity theft block request was denied specifically because the account
> [L17] was locked.
> [L18] This conduct constitutes a clear obstruction of federally protected rights, a violation of
> [L19] multiple FCRA provisions, and a deceptive practice under the FTC Act (UDAP).
> [L20] 1. Unlawful Denial of Access – FCRA § 609(a) Violation
> [L21] The Fair Credit Reporting Act guarantees the consumer's right to access all information in
> [L22] their credit file. That access must be:
> [L23] ●
> [L24] ●
> [L25] ●
> [L26] ●
> [L27] Full
> [L28] Timely
> [L29] Functional
> [L30] Not arbitrarily restricted
> [L31] Phone-only access is not sufficient, especially when:
> [L32] ●
> [L33] ●
> [L34] ●
> [L35] ●
> [L36] I cannot review the full contents of my file
> [L37] I cannot upload documentation or evidence for disputes
> [L38] I cannot submit, manage, or track 605B disputes or freezes
> [L39] I cannot review or confirm actions taken
> [L40] The continued denial of online access constitutes a material obstruction of my rights under §
> [L41] 609(a).
> [L42] 2. Using a Fraud / Identity Theft Lock to Deny a § 605B Identity Theft Block -
> [L43] FCRA § 605B Violation
> [L44] TransUnion has claimed that because of a fraud-related lock on my file, they are “not allowed”
> [L45] or “not able” to process my § 605B block request. This is false and not supported anywhere in
> [L46] the law.
> [L47] FCRA § 605B requires:
> [L48] A CRA must block information identified by the consumer as resulting from identity
> [L49] theft within 4 business days, upon receipt of:
> [L50] ●
> [L51] ●
> [L52] ●
> [L53] A valid FTC report
> [L54] Proof of identity and address
> [L55] A written dispute
> [L56] There is no exception in the FCRA for accounts that are “locked” or flagged due to fraud. On
> [L57] the contrary, these protections are specifically designed for identity theft victims.
> [L58] To deny a 605B block because the account is locked due to identity theft is not just circular
> [L59] reasoning — it is deliberate, systemic obstruction of federal rights.
> [L60] 3. Refusal to Unlock the Account – Procedural Interference with § 611 and §
> [L61] 1681e(a)
> [L62] I have explicitly requested that my account be unlocked to:
> [L63] ●
> [L64] ●
> [L65] ●
> [L66] ●
> [L67] View my full report
> [L68] Submit disputes
> [L69] Review investigation outcomes
> [L70] Use freeze and fraud alert features
> [L71] TransUnion has refused to unlock the account, citing internal security rules and policies. This
> [L72] refusal directly interferes with my ability to:
> [L73] ●
> [L74] ●
> [L75] ●
> [L76] Dispute inaccuracies under § 611
> [L77] Ensure file accuracy under § 1681e(a)
> [L78] Maintain proper fraud protection under § 605A
> [L79] 4. Deceptive Practices – FTC Act § 5 (UDAP)
> [L80] By claiming that:
> [L81] ●
> [L82] ●
> [L83] ●
> [L84] ●
> [L85] My 605B request was denied because of a fraud lock
> [L86] I cannot access my own credit file online
> [L87] Deletion letters are required from creditors (false)
> [L88] Internal “policy” overrides the statute
> [L89] …TransUnion has engaged in unfair and deceptive acts or practices (UDAP) under the FTC
> [L90] Act § 5.

> [L43] 3. Failed to provide reasons for denial – in violation of § 611(a)(6)(B)(iii)

> [L45] FTC states they do NOT expire; and 1 claim per FTC report at a time”) unsupported by law
> [L46] 5. Repeatedly refused corrective action despite recorded supervisory confirmation of receipt

> [L48] ● Willful noncompliance (§ 1681n)
> [L49] ● Reckless disregard for FCRA obligations
> [L50] ● Negligent handling under § 1681o
> [L51] Systemic procedural abuse not based in statute or regulation

> [L56] § 1681n(a)(3) Reckless Disregard Punitive damages (TBD at trial)

> [L61] Maximum (with punitive): $50,000.00+

> [L63] To resolve this matter amicably and avoid formal legal action, I am willing to settle under the following

> [L65] 1.

> Mclick Target: "2:6"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii) (FCRA §611).
> [L2] Willful violations trigger statutory and punitive damages under §1681n. Negligent violations are
> [L3] actionable under §1681o.
> [L4] Timeline of §605B Submissions (Fully Documented)
> [L5] Since June 5, 2025, I submitted multiple complete §605B identity theft block requests, each
> [L6] via trackable certified mail with full documentation + signature of receipt, including:
> [L7] ● FTC Report
> [L8] ● Notarized Affidavit
> [L9] ● Government ID
> [L10] ● Proof of Address
> [L11] Submission Dates:
> [L12] ● June 9, 2025 (CFPB Complaint 250609-21400333)
> [L13] ● June 13, 2025
> [L14] ● June 16, 2025 (CFPB Complaint #250616-21593904)
> [L15] ● July 4th, 2025 (post CFPB Complaint #250704-22063859)
> [L16] Despite meeting every statutory requirement, TransUnion refused to block fraudulent data
> [L17] within 4 business days as required by §605B(a).
> [L18] Denial Reasons Provided by TransUnion
> [L19] (Calls have been recorded, documented, and transcribed via Minnesota 1 way consent laws.
> [L20] Although as a courtesy, all supervisors were informed beforehand of the recording &
> [L21] documentation of the calls)
> [L22] In calls and responses, TransUnion representatives provided unlawful reasons for refusing my
> [L23] §605B requests:
> [L24] 1. Creditor deletion letters required:
> [L25] 12195064.1.4-10
> [L26] ○ Repeatedly demanded letters from creditors as the “only” way, even though
> [L27] FCRA §605B has no such requirement.
> [L28] 2. “System will not allow” removals:
> [L29] ○ Special Handling Supervisor(s), alleged the system prevented deletion even with
> [L30] valid documentation.
> [L31] 3. Internal policy overrides law:
> [L32] ○ Employees cited “internal policy” to ignore §605B’s 4-day block requirement.
> [L33] 4. Rejecting valid FTC Reports and affidavits:
> [L34] ○ No deficiencies in my documentation were ever identified.
> [L35] ○ Rejecting Valid FTC Reports and Affidavits Without Lawful Justification
> [L36] Despite my submission of a valid FTC Identity Theft Report, sworn
> [L37] affidavit, and all required supporting documents under FCRA §605B,
> [L38] TransUnion has repeatedly rejected my request to block fraudulent
> [L39] inquiries without identifying any specific deficiency in the materials
> [L40] provided - either in writing or during recorded phone calls with supervisors
> [L41] from their Fraud Department.
> [L42] This violates multiple provisions of federal law:
> [L43] 1. FCRA §605B(b)(2):
> [L44] "An identity theft report may be a report to a law enforcement
> [L45] agency or the Federal Trade Commission."
> [L46] This statute explicitly confirms that a FTC Identity Theft Report
> [L47] (Affidavit) is legally valid for identity theft blocking requests. It does not
> [L48] require a police report, nor may the CRA reject an FTC report based
> [L49] solely on form preference.
> [L50] 2. FCRA §605B(c)(2):
> [L51] "The consumer reporting agency may not refuse to block the
> [L52] information on the grounds that the consumer has not provided any
> [L53] additional documentation or information, other than as described in
> [L54] subsection (b)."
> [L55] Once the FTC Report, affidavit, ID, and proof of address are submitted
> [L56] - which I provided in full - TransUnion is legally prohibited from requiring
> [L57] any additional documents or imposing further barriers.
> [L58] 12195064.1.5-10
> [L59] 3. FCRA §611(a)(6)(B)(iii): Requirement to Identify
> [L60] Basis for Denial
> [L61] If a CRA rejects or modifies a request, they are required to
> [L62] provide a specific explanation for the decision.
> [L63] "If the agency determines that the dispute is frivolous
> [L64] or irrelevant, it shall notify the consumer of the
> [L65] determination not later than 5 business days... and
> [L66] shall include the reasons for the determination, and
> [L67] identify any information required to investigate the
> [L68] disputed information."

> [L48] CFPB Complaint: TransUnion Willful Noncompliance with
> [L49] FCRA 605B Identity Theft Blocking Requirements
> [L50] Subject: Repeated Refusal by TransUnion to Block Unauthorized Inquiries Under FCRA 605B
> [L51] Despite Receiving All Legally Required Documentation
> [L52] Summary of Issue:
> [L53] TransUnion has willfully and repeatedly violated FCRA § 605B by refusing to block
> [L54] unauthorized inquiries from my credit report, despite having received all federally required
> [L55] documentation. Their repeated denials contradict the plain language of the law, constitute
> [L56] reckless disregard, and result in ongoing consumer harm.
> [L57] Key Facts and Timeline:
> [L58] June 5th and June 11th, 2025: I submitted all necessary documentation required by FCRA §
> [L59] 605B to TransUnion, including:
> [L60] ●
> [L61] Valid FTC Identity Theft Report
> [L62] ●
> [L63] Notarized Affidavit declaring the inquiries were unauthorized
> [L64] ●
> [L65] Written Statement fully detailing the identity theft
> [L66] ●
> [L67] Government-issued ID and supporting verification
> [L68] TransUnion acknowledged receipt and validity of these documents multiple times, yet
> [L69] continues to deny the blocking of inquiries.
> [L70] Instead, they claim they are "unable to remove inquiries this way" and only offer to "notify
> [L71] the creditors,
> [L72] " which does not fulfill their legal obligation under § 605B to block the information
> [L73] within 4 business days.
> [L74] I have directly spoken with four different supervisors over the past 11 days, all of whom have
> [L75] refused to comply, citing undisclosed “internal policies” that contradict federal law and are
> [L76] not disclosed upon request.
> [L77] Violations Committed:
> [L78] ●
> [L79] FCRA § 605B(a) – Failure to block disputed information within 4 business days upon
> [L80] receipt of valid documents.
> [L81] ●
> [L82] ●
> [L83] ●
> [L84] FCRA § 605B(e) – Willful refusal to block information after receiving all required
> [L85] documentation.
> [L86] 15 U.S. Code § 1681n – Civil liability for willful noncompliance; includes actual
> [L87] damages, punitive damages, and attorneys’ fees.

---

# MASTER VIOLATION COUNT LEDGER  
(Chronological • Event-Based • Statute-Tied • Fine-Specified)

---

## LEGAL COUNTING RULE (APPLIED CONSISTENTLY)

A **separate violation count** is assessed **each time** all three occur:

1. You submitted a **complete FCRA §605B identity theft packet**  
2. TransUnion **had a mandatory legal duty** to act  
3. TransUnion **refused, delayed, obstructed, or misrepresented compliance**

Counts are **not duplicated**, **not inflated**, and **not speculative**.  
Each count corresponds to a **specific date, call, letter, or deadline failure**.

---

## KEY STATUTORY PENALTIES (REFERENCE)

- **Willful / Reckless Noncompliance** → 15 U.S.C. §1681n  
  - $1,000 **per violation** + punitive damages + fees
- **Negligent Noncompliance** → 15 U.S.C. §1681o  
  - Actual damages + fees
- **FTC Act §5 (UDAP)**  
  - Separate regulatory penalties (not capped here)

---

# SECTION A — §605B FAILURE TO BLOCK (CORE VIOLATIONS)

**Statute:** 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)  
**Duty:** Block disputed identity-theft information within **4 business days**  
**Fine:** $1,000 per willful violation (§1681n)

### COUNT A1–A4 — Initial Missed Statutory Deadlines
- **Event:** Complete §605B packets submitted
- **Dates:** June 9, June 13, June 16, July 4, 2025
- **Violation:** No block within 4 business days
- **Mental State:** Willful (post-notice, repeated)

**Counts:** 4  
**Exposure:** $4,000

---

### COUNT A5–A8 — Continued Reporting After Deadline
- **Event:** Credit file continued reporting disputed data weeks later
- **Source:** Credit reports + supervisor admissions
- **Violation:** Ongoing refusal to perform completed duty
- **Mental State:** Willful / Reckless

**Counts:** 4  
**Exposure:** $4,000

---

## SECTION B — ILLEGAL DOCUMENTATION DEMANDS

**Statute:** §605B(c)(2)  
**Prohibition:** CRA may **not require anything beyond** FTC report, ID, address, statement  
**Fine:** $1,000 per willful violation

### COUNT B1–B6 — Creditor Deletion Letter Demands
- **Dates:** Multiple calls (June 30 + others)
- **Source:** Hazel Smith, Derek, Frank, Fraud Dept calls
- **Conduct:** “We need a creditor letter”
- **Mental State:** Willful (law read aloud, still refused)

**Counts:** 6  
**Exposure:** $6,000

---

## SECTION C — FALSE “SYSTEM / POLICY” EXCUSES

**Statutes:**  
- §605B(a) (mandatory block)  
- FTC Act §5 (deceptive practice)

### COUNT C1–C5 — “System Will Not Allow It”
- **Event:** Supervisors admit technical/policy refusal
- **Source:** Recorded calls
- **Violation:** Internal policy overriding federal law
- **Mental State:** Willful / Reckless

**Counts:** 5  
**Exposure:** $5,000 (+ FTC penalties)

---

## SECTION D — FAILURE TO ISSUE REQUIRED DENIAL NOTICES

**Statute:** §611(a)(6)(B)(iii)  
**Duty:** If denying, must explain **why** and **how to cure**  
**Fine:** $1,000 per willful violation

### COUNT D1–D5 — No Written Reasons Provided
- **Event:** Vague “not acceptable” responses
- **Source:** Mail responses + calls
- **Violation:** No explanation, no cure instructions
- **Mental State:** Willful

# SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS  
## (CHRONOLOGICAL & EVENT-BASED)

### 5.1 Counting Method (Plain Language)

A statutory violation is counted **each time** TransUnion:

1. **Received a complete and compliant FCRA §605B identity-theft submission or notice**, and  
2. **Failed to perform a mandatory legal duty**, including but not limited to:
   - Blocking disputed information within four (4) business days  
   - Refraining from unlawful documentation demands  
   - Providing lawful, specific written denial reasons  
   - Explaining how to cure an alleged deficiency  
   - Conducting a reasonable investigation  
   - Maintaining reasonable accuracy  
   - Providing access to the consumer file  

Each failure is a **separate, independently finable violation** under the Fair Credit Reporting Act.

No estimates, multipliers, or assumptions are used.  
Each count corresponds to a **specific date, event, duty, and failure**.

---

Legal Standard (FCRA §605B and Related Provisions)
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a) (FCRA §605B), CRAs must block fraudulent information within
four (4) business days upon receiving:

Willful violations trigger statutory and punitive damages under §1681n. Negligent violations are
actionable under §1681o.

Despite my submission of a valid FTC Identity Theft Report, sworn

This violates multiple provisions of federal law:
1. FCRA §605B(b)(2):
"An identity theft report may be a report to a law enforcement
agency or the Federal Trade Commission."
This statute explicitly confirms that a FTC Identity Theft Report
(Affidavit) is legally valid for identity theft blocking requests. It does not
require a police report, nor may the CRA reject an FTC report based
solely on form preference.
2. FCRA §605B(c)(2):
"The consumer reporting agency may not refuse to block the
information on the grounds that the consumer has not provided any
additional documentation or information, other than as described in
subsection (b)."
Once the FTC Report, affidavit, ID, and proof of address are submitted

any additional documents or imposing further barriers.

If a CRA rejects or modifies a request, they are required to
provide a specific explanation for the decision.
"If the agency determines that the dispute is frivolous
or irrelevant, it shall notify the consumer of the
determination not later than 5 business days... and
shall include the reasons for the determination, and
identify any information required to investigate the
disputed information."

# SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS  
## (CHRONOLOGICAL & NUMERICALLY DEFINENSIBLE)

### 5.1 Counting Method (Plain English — Court Safe)

A statutory violation is counted **each time TransUnion**:

1. **Received a complete and compliant FCRA §605B identity theft submission or notice**, and  
2. **Had a distinct legal duty triggered**, and  
3. **Failed to perform that duty**.

Each duty arises **independently** under federal law.  
Each failure is **separately actionable and finable**.

Counts are based on:
- Documented submissions
- Recorded calls with admissions
- Account access obstruction
- Continued reporting after notice
- Executive-level notice with no cure

No counts are based on:
- Number of inquiries  
- Emotional impact  
- Internal multipliers  

This method is **conservative, event-based, and statute-specific**.

---

### 5.2 Liability-Triggering Events (Documented)

The following **five written submissions** and **subsequent recorded calls** are fully documented by certified mail receipts, CFPB records, and call transcripts:

1. **June 9, 2025** — Initial §605B submission  
2. **June 13, 2025** — Second compliant submission  
3. **June 16, 2025** — Third submission + CFPB complaint  
4. **July 4, 2025** — Fourth submission + CFPB escalation  
5. **August 7, 2025** — CEO-level certified notice (no response)  
6. **Multiple recorded calls (June–August 2025)** — Admissions and refusals  
7. **Ongoing account lockout** — Obstruction of statutory rights

Each event independently triggered mandatory statutory duties.

---

### 5.3 Core Duties That Applied at Each Event

At each submission or notice, TransUnion was legally required to:

1. Block fraudulent information within four (4) business days — **§1681c-2(a)**  
2. Refrain from demanding unauthorized documentation — **§1681c-2(c)(2)**  
3. Provide a specific written denial explanation if refusing — **§1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)**  
4. Explain how to cure any alleged deficiency — **§1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)**  
5. Maintain reasonable accuracy after notice — **§1681e(b)**  
6. Conduct a reasonable investigation when applicable — **§1681i**  
7. Provide access to the consumer file — **§1681g**

Failure of **any one duty** creates a separate violation.

SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)
1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)
I was the victim of identity theft.
Federal law provides clear, mandatory protections to identity theft victims.
I followed the law exactly.
TransUnion did not.
Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:
Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries
Invented documentation requirements that do not exist in law
Misrepresented federal law
Admitted on recorded calls that no investigation occurred
Locked me out of my own credit file
Continued reporting information it knew was fraudulent for months
This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)
I respectfully request:
Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)
Restoration of full TransUnion account access under 15 U.S.C. §1681g
Written confirmation of:
Inquiry deletions
Account restoration
Staff retraining on FCRA §605B & FCRA Compliance
Elimination of internal policies or system constraints overriding federal law
Confidential settlement of $75,000–$100,000, reflecting the documented violations and damages.


This proposal is made without prejudice and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.

Proposed Acceptance Language
“TransUnion acknowledges receipt of this demand. As resolution, we have removed the disputed inquiries, restored account access, retrained applicable staff for compliance, and offer a confidential financial settlement of $____ in full satisfaction of all claims.”


SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES
2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under FCRA §605B
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, upon receipt of:
An FTC Identity Theft Report
Proof of identity
Proof of address
A written statement identifying fraudulent items
A consumer reporting agency must block the fraudulent information within four (4) business days.
There is:
No discretion
No investigation prerequisite
No creditor permission requirement
This duty is mandatory and absolute.

## ✅ COPY-PASTE INSERT (CEO-LEVEL, CLASS-ACTION SAFE)

### **Systemic and Class-Wide Liability Exposure**

The conduct described above is not isolated, discretionary, or specific to my account. It is the direct result of **standardized TransUnion policies, system controls, and scripted procedures** applied uniformly to consumers reporting identity theft under FCRA §605B.

Based on recorded calls, written correspondence, and repeated supervisor statements, TransUnion employees consistently asserted that:
- Creditor deletion letters are required despite no such requirement in federal law;
- Internal systems prevent compliance even when documentation is complete;
- Identity theft victims are routed to “different processes” that delay or defeat statutory rights;
- Account lockouts are permanent and used to deny dispute and blocking rights.

These representations were made **by multiple employees across departments**, including Fraud and Special Handling, and were not framed as case-specific discretion. They were described as **company policy and system limitations**.

Because these procedures are:
- **Uniform**
- **Policy-driven**
- **Centrally implemented**
- **Repeated across interactions**

They necessarily affect **every consumer** who submits a compliant §605B identity theft request and is subjected to the same processes.

This creates **class-wide exposure** under Rule 23 for:
- Willful noncompliance with FCRA §605B and §611;
- Systemic denial of mandatory statutory rights;
- Policy-based obstruction of identity theft remedies;
- Reckless disregard for federal law under *Safeco Ins. Co. v. Burr*.

TransUnion processes **millions of consumer disputes annually**. Even a small percentage of identity theft victims subjected to these same unlawful procedures would result in **extraordinary statutory and punitive exposure**, in addition to regulatory penalties under existing CFPB and FTC consent orders.

This letter is intended to place TransUnion on **actual notice** of:
- Individual liability in my case; and
- **Enterprise-level risk** arising from standardized practices that violate federal consumer protection law.

Failure to correct these policies after executive-level notice materially increases TransUnion’s exposure to:
- Class action litigation;
- Regulatory enforcement;
- Enhanced punitive damages based on willful continuation after notice.

The evidence establishes that TransUnion’s violations arise from **company-wide practices**, including:

• Reliance on internal “policies” to delay or deny FCRA §605B identity theft blocks  
• System limitations cited as justification for refusing statutory duties  
• Uniform demands for creditor letters or additional documentation prohibited by statute  
• Refusal to provide specific written denial reasons as required by FCRA §611  
• Locking consumer accounts under the pretext of “fraud protection,” then using the lock itself to deny statutory rights  
• Admissions by supervisors that no investigation occurred and that compliance was “not possible” due to internal systems  

These justifications were repeated verbatim across multiple calls, supervisors, and departments, demonstrating **standardized operational behavior**, not individual error.

Such conduct satisfies the legal criteria for systemic and class-wide liability because:

• The violations stem from **common policies and practices**  
• The same unlawful procedures are applied to all similarly situated consumers  
• Individual circumstances are irrelevant to the violations  
• Liability can be established through common proof (policies, scripts, recordings, training materials)

Courts consistently hold that reliance on internal policy or technical limitations to defeat FCRA obligations constitutes **willful noncompliance as a matter of law**.

This section is included to establish **pattern, practice, foreseeability, and willfulness**, and to preserve class-wide exposure arising from TransUnion’s uniform procedures.  
No independent class claim is asserted herein.

The violations documented in this case arise from standardized TransUnion policies and procedures, not individualized conduct.

However, if these same unlawful practices were applied to other identity-theft-affected consumers — including refusal to honor FCRA §605B submissions, unlawful documentation demands, denial of statutory notices, and account lockouts — TransUnion faces substantial additional exposure.

Public data confirms that TransUnion processes **millions of consumer disputes annually**.  
Even conservative replication of these violations across a limited subset of similarly situated consumers would result in significant aggregate liability.

SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)
1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)
I was the victim of identity theft.
Federal law provides clear, mandatory protections to identity theft victims.
I followed the law exactly.
TransUnion did not.
Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:
Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries
Invented documentation requirements that do not exist in law
Misrepresented federal law
Admitted on recorded calls that no investigation occurred
Locked me out of my own credit file
Continued reporting information it knew was fraudulent for months
This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)
I respectfully request:
Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)
Restoration of full TransUnion account access under 15 U.S.C. §1681g
Written confirmation of:
Inquiry deletions
Account restoration
Staff retraining on FCRA §605B & FCRA Compliance
Elimination of internal policies or system constraints overriding federal law
Confidential settlement of $75,000–$100,000, reflecting the documented violations and damages.


This proposal is made without prejudice and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.

Proposed Acceptance Language
“TransUnion acknowledges receipt of this demand. As resolution, we have removed the disputed inquiries, restored account access, retrained applicable staff for compliance, and offer a confidential financial settlement of $____ in full satisfaction of all claims.”


SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES
2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under FCRA §605B
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, upon receipt of:
An FTC Identity Theft Report
Proof of identity
Proof of address
A written statement identifying fraudulent items
A consumer reporting agency must block the fraudulent information within four (4) business days.
There is:
No discretion
No investigation prerequisite
No creditor permission requirement
This duty is mandatory and absolute.

SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS
(CHRONOLOGICAL & NUMERICALLY DEFINENSIBLE)
5.1 Counting Method (Plain English — Court Safe)
A statutory violation is counted each time TransUnion:
Received a complete and compliant FCRA §605B identity theft submission or notice, and
Had a distinct legal duty triggered, and
Failed to perform that duty.
Each duty arises independently under federal law.
Each failure is separately actionable and finable.
Counts are based on:
Documented submissions
Recorded calls with admissions
Account access obstruction
Continued reporting after notice
Executive-level notice with no cure
No counts are based on:
Number of inquiries
Emotional impact
Internal multipliers
This method is conservative, event-based, and statute-specific.

5.2 Liability-Triggering Events (Documented)
The following five written submissions and subsequent recorded calls are fully documented by certified mail receipts, CFPB records, and call transcripts:
June 9, 2025 — Initial §605B submission
June 13, 2025 — Second compliant submission
June 16, 2025 — Third submission + CFPB complaint
July 4, 2025 — Fourth submission + CFPB escalation
August 7, 2025 — CEO-level certified notice (no response)
Multiple recorded calls (June–August 2025) — Admissions and refusals
Ongoing account lockout — Obstruction of statutory rights
Each event independently triggered mandatory statutory duties.

5.3 Core Duties That Applied at Each Event
At each submission or notice, TransUnion was legally required to:
Block fraudulent information within four (4) business days — §1681c-2(a)
Refrain from demanding unauthorized documentation — §1681c-2(c)(2)
Provide a specific written denial explanation if refusing — §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)
Explain how to cure any alleged deficiency — §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)
Maintain reasonable accuracy after notice — §1681e(b)
Conduct a reasonable investigation when applicable — §1681i
Provide access to the consumer file — §1681g
Failure of any one duty creates a separate violation.

SECTION 1 — SIMPLE OVERVIEW (READ THIS FIRST)
1.1 What This Case Is About (Plain English)
I was the victim of identity theft.
Federal law provides clear, mandatory protections to identity theft victims.
I followed the law exactly.
TransUnion did not.
Despite receiving complete, notarized, legally sufficient identity theft documentation multiple times, TransUnion:
Refused to remove fraudulent inquiries
Invented documentation requirements that do not exist in law
Misrepresented federal law
Admitted on recorded calls that no investigation occurred
Locked me out of my own credit file
Continued reporting information it knew was fraudulent for months
This conduct violates multiple provisions of the Fair Credit Reporting Act (“FCRA”), repeatedly and knowingly.

1.2 Requested Resolution (Without Prejudice)
I respectfully request:
Deletion of all fraudulent inquiries as required by 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a)
Restoration of full TransUnion account access under 15 U.S.C. §1681g
Written confirmation of:
Inquiry deletions
Account restoration
Staff retraining on FCRA §605B & FCRA Compliance
Elimination of internal policies or system constraints overriding federal law
Confidential settlement of $75,000–$100,000, reflecting the documented violations and damages.


This proposal is made without prejudice and solely to resolve this matter without litigation.

Proposed Acceptance Language
“TransUnion acknowledges receipt of this demand. As resolution, we have removed the disputed inquiries, restored account access, retrained applicable staff for compliance, and offer a confidential financial settlement of $____ in full satisfaction of all claims.”


SECTION 2 — WHAT THE LAW REQUIRES
2.1 Identity Theft Blocking Under FCRA §605B
Under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2, upon receipt of:
An FTC Identity Theft Report
Proof of identity
Proof of address
A written statement identifying fraudulent items
A consumer reporting agency must block the fraudulent information within four (4) business days.
There is:
No discretion
No investigation prerequisite
No creditor permission requirement
This duty is mandatory and absolute.

SECTION 3 — PLAINTIFF’S FULL COMPLIANCE
3.1 Documentation Submitted
Each submission included:
FTC Identity Theft Report
Notarized affidavit
Government photo ID
Social Security card
Proof of address
Written identification of fraudulent inquiries
Certified mail with signature confirmation
No submission was deficient.

3.2 Timeline (Abbreviated)
June 9, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion — CFPB ID + receipt
June 13, 2025 — Certified Mail — TransUnion Legal Department — Signature proof
June 16, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion Fraud Department — Signature proof
July 4, 2025 — CFPB + Mail — TransUnion — Signature proof
August 7, 2025 — Certified Mail — TransUnion President — Signature proof

Each submission independently triggered a statutory duty to block under 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(a).



SECTION 4 — TRANSUNION’S MISCONDUCT
4.1 Failure to Block (§1681c-2)
No blocking within four days
No blocking for months
Continued reporting after notice

4.2 Unlawful Documentation Demands (§1681c-2(c)(2))
Violation of 15 U.S.C. §1681c-2(c)(2)
TransUnion repeatedly demanded:
Creditor letters
Police reports
One-packet-per-inquiry rules
System limitations
All forbidden by statute. Federal law explicitly forbids these demands.

4.3 Misrepresentation of Federal Law (Recorded Audio + Transcriptions)
Supervisors stated:
“We need a creditor letter”
“The system won’t allow it”
“You must contact the creditor”
“We have not investigated”
These statements constitute knowing or reckless misconduct.

4.4 Failure to Provide Denial Explanations (§1681i)
Violation of 15 U.S.C. §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)
TransUnion:
Responded only “documents not acceptable”
Refused to identify deficiencies
Refused to explain how to cure
Cited no statute



4.5 Denial of File Access (§1681g)
Account permanently locked. “There’s nothing you can do to unlock it”
Lock used as justification to deny §605B rights
Access not restored despite identity verification. 

4.6 Policy & System Overrides
TransUnion repeatedly relied on internal policies and technical constraints to override mandatory federal statutory duties.
Reliance on company policy or system limitations to defeat FCRA obligations constitutes willful noncompliance as a matter of law.

4.7 Context of TransUnion’s Data Breach History
TransUnion has suffered multiple widely reported data breaches and cybersecurity incidents involving unauthorized access to consumer data.
This history is relevant because:
It demonstrates longstanding weaknesses in data integrity and safeguards
It heightens TransUnion’s duty to ensure accuracy and prompt correction
Continued reporting of known fraudulent data after notice reflects reckless disregard for consumer rights under 15 U.S.C. §1681e
TransUnion’s publicly reported data breaches establish heightened foreseeability, duty of care, and the importance of accurate, prompt correction after identity theft notice. Continued reporting after notice reflects reckless disregard under §1681e.
This allegation is offered solely to establish foreseeability, duty of care, and recklessness, not to assert independent breach liability.

4.8 Systemic and Class-Wide Noncompliance (Policy-Driven Violations)
The misconduct described above is not isolated, discretionary, or unique to this Plaintiff.
It is the direct result of standardized TransUnion policies, procedures, system constraints, and employee scripting that uniformly override mandatory federal law.
The evidence establishes that TransUnion’s violations arise from company-wide practices, including:
• Reliance on internal “policies” to delay or deny FCRA §605B identity theft blocks
• System limitations cited as justification for refusing statutory duties
• Uniform demands for creditor letters or additional documentation prohibited by statute
• Refusal to provide specific written denial reasons as required by FCRA §611
• Locking consumer accounts under the pretext of “fraud protection,” then using the lock itself to deny statutory rights
• Admissions by supervisors that no investigation occurred and that compliance was “not possible” due to internal systems
These justifications were repeated verbatim across multiple calls, supervisors, and departments, demonstrating standardized operational behavior, not individual error.

Such conduct satisfies the legal criteria for systemic and class-wide liability because:
• The violations stem from common policies and practices
• The same unlawful procedures are applied to all similarly situated consumers
• Individual circumstances are irrelevant to the violations
• Liability can be established through common proof (policies, scripts, recordings, training materials)
Courts consistently hold that reliance on internal policy or technical limitations to defeat FCRA obligations constitutes willful noncompliance as a matter of law.

This section is included to establish pattern, practice, foreseeability, and willfulness, and to preserve class-wide exposure arising from TransUnion’s uniform procedures.
No independent class claim is asserted herein.



SECTION 5 — COUNTED STATUTORY VIOLATIONS
(CHRONOLOGICAL & NUMERICALLY DEFINENSIBLE)
5.1 Counting Method (Plain English — Court Safe)
A statutory violation is counted each time TransUnion:
Received a complete and compliant FCRA §605B identity theft submission or notice, and
Had a distinct legal duty triggered, and
Failed to perform that duty.
Each duty arises independently under federal law.
Each failure is separately actionable and finable.
Counts are based on:
Documented submissions
Recorded calls with admissions
Account access obstruction
Continued reporting after notice
Executive-level notice with no cure
No counts are based on:
Number of inquiries
Emotional impact
Internal multipliers
This method is conservative, event-based, and statute-specific.

5.2 Liability-Triggering Events (Documented)
The following five written submissions and subsequent recorded calls are fully documented by certified mail receipts, CFPB records, and call transcripts:
June 9, 2025 — Initial §605B submission
June 13, 2025 — Second compliant submission
June 16, 2025 — Third submission + CFPB complaint
July 4, 2025 — Fourth submission + CFPB escalation
August 7, 2025 — CEO-level certified notice (no response)
Multiple recorded calls (June–August 2025) — Admissions and refusals
Ongoing account lockout — Obstruction of statutory rights
Each event independently triggered mandatory statutory duties.

5.3 Core Duties That Applied at Each Event
At each submission or notice, TransUnion was legally required to:
Block fraudulent information within four (4) business days — §1681c-2(a)
Refrain from demanding unauthorized documentation — §1681c-2(c)(2)
Provide a specific written denial explanation if refusing — §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)
Explain how to cure any alleged deficiency — §1681i(a)(6)(B)(iii)
Maintain reasonable accuracy after notice — §1681e(b)
Conduct a reasonable investigation when applicable — §1681i
Provide access to the consumer file — §1681g
Failure of any one duty creates a separate violation.

SECTION 7 — DAMAGES
7.1 Statutory Damages (§1681n)
Authority: 15 U.S.C. §1681n
52 violations × $1,000 per violation
Total Statutory Damages: $52,000

7.2 Actual Damages
Including, but not limited to:
Credit harm
Loss of time
Emotional distress
Administrative and compliance burden
Conservative Estimated Range: $25,000 – $40,000

7.3 Punitive Damages

Punitive damages are warranted because the misconduct was:
Repeated
Post-notice
Policy-driven
Admitted on recorded calls
Courts routinely apply multipliers in such circumstances.


Policy-driven, post-notice misconduct justifies punitive damages.
Conservative multiplier (3×): $52,000 × 3 = $156,000
Punitive damages are authorized under 15 U.S.C. §1681n(a)(2).

7.4 Total Exposure
Estimated Total Exposure:
Statutory Damages: $52,000
Actual Damages: $25,000–$40,000
Punitive Damages (3× statutory): $156,000
Total Estimated Exposure:
$233,000 – $248,000, exclusive of attorneys’ fees and costs, which are independently recoverable under the FCRA.

7.5 Systemic and Class-Wide Exposure (Contextual Only)
The violations documented in this case arise from standardized TransUnion policies and procedures, not individualized conduct.
Accordingly, the exposure outlined above reflects only Plaintiff-specific damages.
However, if these same unlawful practices were applied to other identity-theft-affected consumers — including refusal to honor FCRA §605B submissions, unlawful documentation demands, denial of statutory notices, and account lockouts — TransUnion faces substantial additional exposure.
Public data confirms that TransUnion processes millions of consumer disputes annually.
Even conservative replication of these violations across a limited subset of similarly situated consumers would result in significant aggregate liability.
For context only:
• 10,000 affected consumers × $1,000 statutory damages = $10,000,000
• 50,000 affected consumers × $1,000 statutory damages = $50,000,000
• Punitive damages (2–5× statutory) substantially increase exposure
Courts have imposed large judgments for systemic FCRA violations involving standardized practices, including Ramirez v. TransUnion LLC, where class-wide liability was affirmed based on uniform procedures.
This section is provided solely to demonstrate foreseeability, scale, and willfulness, and to contextualize TransUnion’s regulatory, litigation, and settlement exposure.
It does not assert a class action claim, which is expressly reserved.
